Synthetic fibered ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category theory
Ulrik Buchholtz, Jonathan Weinberger

Abstract
We study cocartesian fibrations in the setting of the synthetic ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category theory developed in simplicial type theory introduced by Riehl and Shulman. Our development culminates in a Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian fibrations.



\amsclass

03B38;18N60;18N45;18D30;18N55;55U35;18N50;18D40;18D70

\pubYear
2022

keywords
homotopy type theory, simplicial type theory,
(\infty,1)
-categories, Segal spaces, Rezk spaces, cartesian fibrations

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation and overview

1.1.1
Synthetic mathematics in homotopy type theory

So far, homotopy type theory (HoTT) has served as a convenient framework for a lot of homotopical mathematics. It allows one to reason synthetically about homotopy types, for example to study homotopy or cohomology groups
[
82
,
9
,
67
]
.
Indeed, it functions as an internal language for higher toposes.
This had been conjectured for some time by Awodey, and then recently established
by Shulman
[
70
]
.
Alas, developing specifically the field of higher category theory in standard Book HoTT has not been as fruitful yet. For example, it is still an important open problem to give a definition of the notion of
(\infty,1)
-category completely internal to HoTT. Currently, one has to rely on some extension such as two-level type theory
[
2
]
.

It is also not possible to directly interpret types as higher categories, since not all functors are exponentiable. At the level of model structures presenting the homotopy theory of
(\infty,1)
-categories (such as the Joyal or Rezk model structure), this manifests itself in their failure to be right proper.

1.1.2
Synthetic higher category theory in simplicial homotopy type theory

Outside the realm of Book HoTT, there do exist various approaches to reason type-theoretically about higher categorical structues, cf.
[
13
]
for an overview and discussion.
As one solution, simplicial (homotopy) type theory (sHoTT) has been suggested by Riehl–Shulman
[
61
]
. Also independently proposed by Joyal, the idea is to work internally to simplicial spaces, or more generally, simplicial objects in any higher topos. This extension of HoTT comes with two new kinds of gadgets: shapes and extension types, both with
judgmental equalities
. The strict shape layer encompasses e.g. the standard
n
-simplices
\Delta^{n}
, boundaries
\partial\Delta^{n}
, and horns
\Lambda^{n}_{k}
. The strict extension types correspond to subtypes of dependent function types whose elements
judgmentally
restrict to a fixed section defined on a subshape of a larger shape. These extension types make it possibly to define e.g., the directed hom-types
\hom_{A}(x,y)
, for types
A
and elements
x,y:A
, derived from the arrow type
A^{\Delta^{1}}
. Importantly, extension types are homotopically and computationally well-behaved: they allow for strict computations while maintaining fibrancy.

The prime model of sHoTT is the
(\infty,1)
-topos
{\mathrm{sSpace}}:=\left[{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}^{\Op},{\mathrm{Space}}\right]
of simplicial
\infty
-groupoids, hence sHoTT can be understood as a synthetic theory of simplicial
\infty
-groupoids. The internal simplicial structure of each type
A
can be probed by investigating the function type
\Delta^{n}\to A
. This allows for reasoning about higher categories in a convenient way because one can state the conditions for a type to be groupoidal (
aka discrete
) or (complete) Segal in relatively simple and
finitary
terms using extension types, as shown by Joyal in the classical set-theoretic setting. Semantically, these, in fact correspond to the desired properties, cf.
[
61
, Appendix A]
.

1.1.3
Synthetic fibered higher category theory

In this paper, we complement Riehl–Shulman’s work on
covariant families
,
i.e.
, functorial type families with groupoidal fibers, by a development of a synthetic notion of
cocartesian family
,
i.e.
, functorial type families with categorical fibers. That is, cocartesian families represent functors to the category of categories. Dually, we obtain a synthetic notion of
cartesian family
as well, representing contravariant functors to the category of categories.

Specifically, we give characterizations of cocartesian fibrations via certain adjointness conditions, one of which being the so-called
Chevalley criterion
which traditionally is known from
2
-category theory due to Street
[
80
]
and Gray
[
23
]
, and in the past years has been recovered in Riehl–Verity’s model-independent higher category theory
[
63
]
. We furthermore prove a type-theoretic
2
-Yoneda Lemma, which for
\infty
-cosmoses has been established by
[
64
]
,
[
63
, Section 5.7]
(in turn being inspired by
[
79
]
). This implies the discrete case as a corollary, which previously has been formulated and proven in the type-theoretic setting in
[
61
]
, and semantically for (complete) Segal spaces in
[
32
,
57
]
.

The type-theoretic Yoneda Lemma can also be understood as an induction principle for
directed
arrows. Furthermore, we establish various theorems about cocartesian functors, analogous to
[
63
]
, including characterization theorems in terms of invertibility of mates, as well as several closure properties inspired by
\infty
-cosmos theory. In fact, these closure properties could be understood as completeness results about the
(\infty,1)
-category of small cocartesian fibrations w.r.t.
(\infty,1)
-categorical limits. However, in the work at hand the universal properties will be spelled out explicitly since the universe type in consideration will not itself be an
(\infty,1)
-category. For previous discussions on more structured universes and the Directed Univalence Axiom, cf. Cavallo–Riehl–Sattler
[
60
]
and Weaver–Licata
[
85
]
.
In the context of the latter, Licata has an Agda formalization of the definition of cocartesian families
[
35
]
.

Prior to these main developments, we also briefly touch on non-functorial families of synthetic (pre-)
(\infty,1)
-categories. These notions, admittedly of somewhat auxiliary character, primarily serve to conceptually systematize our developments in the realm of simplicial types. At the end of the paper, we also provide some tie-ins with the pre-established theory of covariant families from
[
61
]
, recovering the familiar characterization of covariant families among cocartesian families.

1.2
Contributions

We re-develop the basic theory of fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories in (a mild extension of) the synthetic setting of Riehl–Shulman’s
simplicial homotopy type theory

[
61
]
. Specifically, we prove several closure properties, characterization theorems, and a Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian fibrations and cocartesian functors. Along the way, we also discuss non-functorial families of (pre-)
(\infty,1)
-categories as auxiliary notions.

Notably, Riehl–Verity’s model-independent higher category theory in
\infty
-cosmoses
[
63
]
has been serving as a principal guiding stone for our developments. Tying in with previous work by Riehl–Shulman, we establish additional characterizations of discrete covariant fibrations and left adjoint right inverse (LARI) adjunctions.

To argue that our synthetic theory in fact captures the well-known analytic theory of fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories, we briefly point out the semantics in (a suitable presentation of) the
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial spaces (or, more generally, simplicial objects in an
(\infty,1)
-topos). However, a detailed semantic discussion is omitted in this text.

1.3
Structure of the paper

After the introductory
Section

1
, we give in
Section

2
an exposition of Riehl–Shulman’s simplicial type theory
[
61
]
and our concrete type-theoretic setup.

A1, A2
Section 3
A3
Section 4
Section 5

Figure 1:
Dependency of the sections

In the subsequent two sections we set the stage for our study of synthetic cocartesian fibrations which makes up the main part of the paper. Namely, in
Section

3
we discuss closure properties of two kinds of “fibrations” which formally generalize cocartesian fibrations and their discrete analogue.
Section

4
captures
non-functorial
families of synthetic (pre-)
(\infty,1)
-categories. Similar to (for instance) Joyal and Lurie’s
inner
(or
mid
)
fibrations
, we discuss these somewhat auxiliary notions of fibrations since at the most general level, types are not necessarily categories.
1
1

1

Rather, they are (Reedy fibrant) simplicial objects.

Afterwards, in
Section

5
, cocartesian fibrations are introduced and discussed, along with cocartesian functors. The development closely parallels Riehl–Verity’s theory of cocartesian fibrations in
\infty
-cosmoses
[
63
, Chapter 5]
. The relations of our setup to discrete covariant fibrations, previously introduced by Riehl–Shulman, are discussed in
Section

6
. As an application of our theory we present a Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian fibrations in
Section

7
, which type-theoretically can be viewed as a principle of
directed arrow induction
for functorial families of Rezk types. This is a direct generalization of Riehl–Shulman’s discrete Yoneda Lemma in
[
61
, Section 9]
, and at the same time a type-theoretic analogue of Riehl–Verity’s Yoneda Lemma in
[
63
, Section 5.7]
.

Appendices
A
and
B
cover basic results on left adjoint right inverse (LARI) adjunctions and mates, inspired by
[
61
, Section 11]
and
[
63
, Appendix B.3/4]
.

In
Figure

1
we outline the logical dependency of the sections with suggested reading orders. Readers primarily interested in the results about cocartesian fibrations and the Yoneda Lemma are invited to follow the path indicated by squiggly arrows “
\rightsquigarrow
”. This is also recommended for a first skim through the paper focusing on the main parts of the theory, avoiding some of the more technical aspects.
A complete tour through the main text is given by straight arrows “
\rightarrow
”, extendable by side trips through the appendices as indicated by dashed arrows “
\dashrightarrow
”.

Related work

This article extends the theory of discrete fibrations in the setting of
Riehl–Shulman’s type theory for synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories
[

, Section 8]

to cover various more general notions of fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories,
building on Riehl–Verity’s theory of co-/cartesian fibrations in
\infty
-cosmoses,
cf.
[

, Chapter 5]
and
[

,

[

and Street
[

Segal and Rezk spaces have been studied by Rezk
[

[

[

[

, Stenzel
[

[

[

[

]

Originally, the theory of Grothendieck
aka

(\infty,1)
-categories (implemented as quasi-categories) has been developed by Joyal
[

as well as Lurie
[

Specifically in the case of simplicial type theory an account to directed univalence has been given by Cavallo–Riehl–Sattler

2
Exposition of Riehl–Shulman’s synthetic

(\infty,1)
-category theory

We recall some basic features and results from Riehl–Shulman’s synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory
[
61
]
, at a very brief and informal level. A significantly more thorough treatment is provided in the original paper.

2.1
Simplicial type theory

2.1.1
Shapes

In terms of homotopy theory, the shape layer enables us to reason about generating anodyne cofibrations using strict equalities.

In simplicial HoTT, next to the familiar layer of (univalent) intensional Martin-Löf type theory, there are new “non-fibrant” layers added that provide a logical calculus of geometric shapes. We start of from the
cube layer
,
i.e.
, a Lawvere theory generated by a single bi-pointed object
0,1:\mathbbn{2}
, the
standard
1
-cube
. A cube context

\Xi\equiv[I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}]

is thus a finite list of cubes

I_{m}\penalty\ \mathrm{cube}

for
1\leq m\leq k
.

On top of the cube layer, we can form
topes
through logical comprehension via (intuitionistic) conjunction
\land
, disjunction
\lor
, and equality
\equiv
. The
tope layer
hence captures sub-polytopes of
n
-cubes (with explicit embedding). A tope formula
\varphi(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
together with a cube context
\Xi\equiv[I_{1},\ldots,I_{k},\vec{J}]
gives rise to a tope

\Xi\vdash\varphi\penalty\ \mathrm{tope}.

The interval
\mathbbn{2}
under consideration shall also come equipped with an
inequality
tope

x,y:\mathbbn{2}\vdash x\leq y\penalty\ \mathrm{tope}

making it a total order (w.r.t. the
strict
equality tope
\equiv
) with
0
and
1
as the bottom and top element, respectively.
2
2

2

For a comparison with the setup of
cubical type theory

[
19
]
cf.
[
61
, Remark 3.2]
.

In particular, we also sometimes make use of
connections
on the cube terms as discussed in
[
61
, Proposition 3.5]
.

A cube together with a tope is called a
shape
:

\frac{I\,\mathrm{cube}\qquad t:I\vdash\varphi\,\mathrm{tope}}{\{t:I\mid\varphi\}\,}

As an addition to the original theory by Riehl–Shulman, we will moreover coerce all shapes to be types, cf.
Section

2.4
. This is still in accordance with the intended class of models.

2.1.2
Extension types

In addition to the strict layers, the other new feature of simplicial type theory is a new type former called the
extension type
, the idea of which originally was due to Lumsdaine and Shulman. Given a shape
\Psi
and a type family
P:\Gamma\to\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
together with a
partial
section
a:\prod_{\Gamma\times\Phi}P
, where
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
denotes a subshape, we can form the corresponding family of extension types

\Gamma\vdash\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle

which is interpreted as a (strict) pullback, cf.
[
61
, Theorem A.16]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Phi}\times_{(\Gamma\times\Psi)^{\Phi}}(\Gamma\times\Psi)^{\Psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\overline{a},\overline{\id_{\Gamma\times\Psi}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means, the elements of
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
are total sections
b:\prod_{\Gamma\times\Psi}P
such that
b|_{\Phi}\equiv a
holds judgmentally:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\Psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

The type-theoretic rules are analogous to the familiar rules of
\Pi
-types, but with the desired judgmental equalities added, cf.
[
61
, Figure 4]
.

In particular, non-dependent instances give rise to function types
A^{\Phi}
where
\Phi
is a shape rather than a type (even though, later on all of our shapes are assumed to be fibrant, cf. Subsection
2.4
). Semantically, this reflects the fact that the intended model is cotensored over simplicial sets, cf. also the discussion in
[
61
, Appendix A]
.

From the given rules one can show that the extension types interact well with the usual
\Pi
- and
\Sigma
-types, as shown in
[
61
, Subsections 4.1, 4.2]
. In particular, there is a version of the type-theoretic axiom of choice involving extension types that will be used a lot.

Theorem
\thetheorem

(Type-theoretic axiom of choice for extension types,
[
61
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
be a shape inclusion. Suppose we are given families
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:\prod_{t:\Psi}(P(t)\to\mathcal{U})
and sections
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(t)
,
b:\prod_{t:\Phi}Q(t,a(t))
. Then there is an equivalence

\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}\sum_{x:P(t)}Q(t,x)\middle|^{\Phi}_{\lambda t.\langle a(t),b(t)\rangle}\right\rangle\simeq\sum_{f:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}Q(t,f(t))\middle|^{\Phi}_{b}\right\rangle.

A further important principle is
relative function extensionality
, which is added as an axiom:

Axiom
\thetheorem

(Relative function extensionality,
[
61
, Axiom 4.6]
)
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
be a shape inclusion.
Given a family
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
such that each
P(t)
is contractible,
and a partial section
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(t)
, then the extension type
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
is contractible.

An important consequence is the
homotopy extension property (HEP)
:

Proposition
\thetheorem

(Homotopy extension property (HEP),
[
61
, Proposition 4.10]
)
.

Fix a shape inclusion
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
. Let
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
be family,
b:\prod_{t:\Psi}P(t)
a total section, and
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}A(t)
a partial section. Then, given a homotopy
H:\prod_{t:\Phi}a(t)=b(t)
, there exist totalizations
a^{\prime}:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
and
H^{\prime}:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}a^{\prime}(t)=b(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{H}\right\rangle
.

2.1.3
Semantics in simplicial spaces

A model of simplicial type theory is given by the Reedy model structure on bisimplicial sets, which presents the
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial spaces. The main steps in proving this are discussed in
[
61
, Appendix A]
, with previous work done in
[
71
,
17
]
. The splitting of the extension type former is carried out in
[
88
]
. In fact, it is conceivable that one can replace the base by an arbitrary (Grothendieck)
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathscr{E}
so that the results developed synthetically will hold for Rezk objects (
i.e.
, internal
(\infty,1)
-categories, cf. e.g.
[
38
,
21
,
74
,
54
]
and
[
63
, Proposition E.3.7]
) in
\mathscr{E}
, also as explained in
ibid
. One should note that the definitions and constructions we are presenting always have characterizations in terms of basic notions, such as (fibered) weak equivalences, (LARI) adjunctions, etc. (See for instance the characterizations of cocartesian fibrations and functors via
Theorems

5.8
,
5.10
and
5.34
)
By Riehl–Verity’s results on model-independence and Rasekh’s work on simplicial and (complete) Segal spaces one can systematically argue that, in essence, all of our internal notions externalize to their intended semantic counterparts—at least when restricting to the Rezk types, which are our objects of primary interest after all.

2.2
Synthetic higher categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textstyle\Delta^{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\Lambda_{1}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\Delta^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}}

Figure 2:
Some shapes

Via the inequality tope of the interval
\mathbbn{2}
we can define simplices and subshapes familiar from simplicial homotopy theory. The first few low-dimensional simplices are given by

\displaystyle\Delta^{0}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbf{1}\;|\;\top\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta^{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbbn{2}\;|\;\top\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\!\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\leq t\}.

The logical connectives of the tope layer enable us to carve out subshapes, such as boundaries and horns, e.g.

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbbn{2}\;|\;t\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},\quad\Lambda_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv t\lor s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\}.

Cf.
Figure

2
for an illustration and
[
61
, Section 3.2]
for a detailed discussion.

Figure 3:
Dependent arrows

We then can define, for any type
B
and fixed elements
b,b^{\prime}:B
the type of arrows from
b
to
b^{\prime}
as

\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\Delta^{1}\to B\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[b,b^{\prime}]}\right\rangle.

Given a type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and an arrow
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
in the base, the type of arrows lying over
u
, from
e:P\,b
to
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
, is given by

{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}P(u(t))\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[e,e^{\prime}]}\right\rangle.

Such an arrow is also called a
dependent arrow
or
dependent homomomorphism
, cf.
Figure

3
.

We will also be considering types of
2
-cells, defined by
3
3

3

The boundary here is given by

[u,v,w]:\partial\Delta^{2}\to B,\quad[u,v,w](t,s)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\begin{cases}u&s\equiv 0\\
v&t\equiv 1\\
w&s\equiv t\end{cases}

and similarly for the dependent case.

\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\equiv\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{2}}_{[u,v,w]}\right\rangle,\quad\hom^{2,P}_{\sigma}(f,g;h)\equiv\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P(\sigma(t,s))\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{2}}_{[f,g,h]}\right\rangle,

see
Figure

4
for an illustration:

Figure 4:
Dependent
2
-cells

We abbreviate

\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\equiv(b\to_{B}b^{\prime})\equiv(b\to b^{\prime})

when the intent is clear from the context. For families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, we write

\hom_{u}^{P}(e,e^{\prime})\equiv(e\to^{P}_{u}e^{\prime}).

For a type
B
, we have two projections from the arrow type, given by evaluation

\partial_{k}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,\quad\partial_{k}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u.u(k),

for
k=0,1
.

Similarly to the notation introduced above, for the type of natural transformations between a fixed pair of functors we abbreviate

\underset{A\to B}{\hom}(f,g)\equiv(f\Rightarrow g).

Sometimes, we also denote the type of
2
-simplices by

\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\equiv(u,v\Rightarrow w),

and likewise for the dependent version.
With these prerequisites, Riehl–Shulman
[
61
]
define a type
B
to be a
Segal type
such that the proposition

\isSegal(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:b\to b^{\prime}\\
v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\Big)

is true. This means that
B
has
weak composition of directed arrows
. After Joyal, the Segal condition can be stated as

\isSegal(B)\simeq\isEquiv(B^{\iota}),

with the inclusion where
\iota:\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
,
i.e.
,
B^{\iota}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(-\circ\iota):B^{\Delta^{2}}\to B^{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}
restricts filled
2
-simplices in
B
onto their
(2,1)
-horn.

Segal types can be thought of as synthetic
pre
-
(\infty,1)
-categories, which here in simplicial homotopy type theory is expressed as a
property
rather than structure, echoing the familiar situation from the semantics in simplicial spaces. As discussed in
[
61
, Section 5]
, Segal types can be endowed with a weak composition
operation
which is weakly associative. For arrows
f:a\to b
,
g:b\to c
in some Segal type
B
, one writes
g\circ f
for the chosen composite arrow.

The
identity arrow
of an element
b:B
is given by the constant map

\id_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.b:\Delta^{1}\to B.

Often, naturality w.r.t. directed arrows comes “for free”. In particular, any function
f:A\to B
between Segal types is a
functor
in the sense that it preserves composition and identities up to propositional equality, as proven in
[
61
, Section 6.1]
. The action of a functor on points already determines its actions on arrows as discussed in
loc. cit
.

Although the semantics is given by a structure presenting an
(\infty,1)
-topos—a certain kind of
(\infty,1)
-category—we, in fact, have access to portions of the
2
-dimensional structure present in the model as well. Since Segal types form an exponential ideal, the type
A\to B
is Segal if
B
is, and this allows us to study natural transformations between functors and lax diagrams of types, cf. Appendix
A
and the groundwork in
[
61
, Section 6]
. This enables us to adapt several developments from Riehl–Verity’s model-independent higher category theory from
\infty
-cosmoses
to type theory. Roughly, an
\infty
-cosmos
4
4

4

The name “
\infty
-cosmos” has been suggested by Peter May
[
63
, Acknowledgments]
, after Street’s (fibrational) cosmoses
[
78
,
77
]
.
is a model for an
(\infty,2)
-categorical universe of a given notion of “
\infty
-category” which could mean
(\infty,n)
-categories (for
0\leq n\leq\infty
) but also e.g., fibrations thereof. This semantical theory will turn out to be an important guiding stone for our internal developments.

Segal types come with two possible notions of isomorphism (analogous to the semantic situation for Segal spaces), the “spatial” one given by propositional equality, and the “categorical” one derived from the directed arrows. Namely, an arrow
f:a\to b
in a Segal type
B
is a (categorical)
isomorphism
if the type

\isIso(f)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{g,h:b\to a}(gf=\id_{a})\times(fh=\id_{b})

is inhabited. As discussed in
[
61
, Section 10]
, the type
\isIso(f)
turns out to be a proposition, so we can define the subtypes

{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(a,b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(a,b)}\isIso(a,b).

In particular, for all
b:B
one has
5
5

5

leaving the witnessing proposition implicit in our notation

\id_{b}:{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(a,b)
.
By path induction we define the comparison map

{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B}:\prod_{a,b:B}(a=_{B}b)\to{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(a,b),\quad{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B,a,a}({\mathrm{refl}}_{a})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{a},

and demanding that this be an equivalence leads to the notion of
Rezk completeness:

\isRezkCompl(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B}).

A
complete Segal type

aka

Rezk type
is a Segal type that is also Rezk complete. A type
B:\mathcal{U}
being Rezk is witnessed by the proposition

\displaystyle{\isRezk}(B)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\isSegal(B)\times\isRezkCompl(B)

\displaystyle\simeq\isEquiv(B^{\iota})\times\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B}).

Then Rezk completeness condition can be understood as a local version of the Univalence Axiom. In the simplicial space model, Rezk types are interpreted as Rezk spaces, which justifies viewing Rezk types as
synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories
. Even though a lot of the development in
[
61
]
actually already works well on the level of (not necessarily complete) Segal types, our study of cocartesian families mostly restricts to complete Segal types, which is in line with preexisting studies of
co-/cartesian fibrations in the higher categorical context
[
28
,
39
,
63
,
52
,
21
,
3
,
6
]
.

Among the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories, we can also consider types that are synthetic
(\infty,0)
-categories,
i.e.
,
\infty
-groupoids. These are called
discrete types
,
6
6

6

semantically known as
Bousfield–Segal spaces
due to Bergner
[
7
, Section 6]
, cf. also earlier work by Bousfield
[
12
]
and more recent work by Stenzel
[
73
]
which refers to the condition that all directed arrows be invertible, namely the comparison map defined inductively by

{\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B}:\prod_{a,b:B}(a=_{B}b)\to\hom_{B}(a,b),\quad{\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B,a,a}({\mathrm{refl}}_{a})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{a},

be an equivalence:

\isDisc(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{a,b:B}\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B,a,b})

In fact, this discreteness condition entails the Rezk condition as shown by Riehl–Shulman. Furthermore, if
B
is a Segal type, for any
a,b:B
, the hom-type
\hom_{B}(a,b)
is discrete by
[
61
, Proposition 8.13]
.

2.3
Covariant families

Riehl–Shulman have introduced the notion of
covariant family
,
i.e.
, families of discrete types varying functorially w.r.t. directed arrows in the base. A type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a (Segal or Rezk) type
B
is
covariant
if

{\mathrm{isCovFam}}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b:B\\
u:a\to b\end{subarray}}\prod_{d:P\,a}\isContr\Big(\sum_{e:P\,b}(d\to^{P}_{u}e)\Big),

i.e.
, arrows in the base can be uniquely lifted w.r.t. a given source vertex.

Semantically, these correspond to
left fibrations
, which encode
(\infty,1)
-copresheaves. Hence, as expected, an example is given, for any
a:B
by the family

\hom_{B}(a,-)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\hom_{B}(a,b):B\to\mathcal{U}.

The central topic of our work is to generalize this study to synthetic
cocartesian fibrations
,
i.e.
, the case where the fibers are Rezk rather than discrete.
7
7

7

Everything dualizes to the case of cartesian fibrations, of course, but we don’t spell this out.

2.4
Fibrant shapes

In the models we are interested in, the shapes will be fibrant objects,
so we may reflect the shape layer into the type layer.
In a generalized algebraic presentation of the syntax,
this will be done with a constructor corresponding to the inference rule:
8
8

8

The introduction, elimination and computation rules state elements of type
\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen
are precisely those of the corresponding shape
I\mid\varphi
. We elide these forms from our notation to ease readability.

\frac{I\,\mathrm{cube}\qquad t:I\vdash\varphi\,\mathrm{tope}}{\lparen t:I\mid\varphi\rparen\,}

In fact, we take the interval
\Delta^{1}
as a type, and the inequality relation
{\leq}:\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}\to\mathrm{Prop}
as a type family, and then all shapes are types using the ordinary type formers.
It seems it should be possible to develop everything we do using standard type theory extended with
(\Delta^{1},{\leq})
along with the postulate that this is an
interval object
,
i.e.
, a totally ordered set with bottom element
0
and top element
1
,
where
0\neq 1
.
This nicely complements the fact that the
1
-topos of simplicial sets is the classifying topos for the geometric theory of a strict interval
[
27
]
.

Recall that if
A
is a type and
B
is a type family over
A
,
then
\prod_{x:A}B(x)
is equivalent to the type of sections of
the first projection
\sum_{x:A}B(x)\to A
.
Similarly, the extension type
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}A(x)\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle

is equivalent to

\sum_{f:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}A(x)}\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}(a\,x=f\,x),

i.e.
, the type of functions over the large shape together with a homotopy
to the given function over the small shape.
Indeed, for any
a
, we have a map from the extension type to the latter type,
so it suffices to show that the induced map on total types,

\left(\sum_{a:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}A(x)}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}A(x)\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle\right)\to\left(\sum_{a:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}A(x)}\sum_{f:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}A(x)}\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}(a\,x=f\,x)\right)

is an equivalence. The codomain here is equivalent to
just
\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}A(x)
(by relative function extensionality),
and the composite maps
\langle a,f\rangle
to
f
. This map has an obvious inverse
that maps
f
to the pair of
f
restricted to
\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen
and
f
.

In particular, this gives the following formulation of (diagrammatic, weak) lifting problems in terms of (formulaic, strict) contractibility statements.

Observation
\thetheorem
.

Consider a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and a shape inclusion
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
. Then, given a total diagram
\sigma:\Psi\to B
with a partial diagram
\kappa:\prod_{\Phi}\sigma^{*}P
lying over,
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

possesses a diagonal filler uniquely up to homotopy if and only if the proposition

\mathrm{isContr}\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{t:\Psi}P(\sigma(s))\Big|^{\Phi}_{\kappa}\Big\rangle\Big)

is inhabited.

Example
\thetheorem
.

Recall from
[
61
]
that a type
A
is Segal precisely if
A\to\mathbf{1}
is right orthogonal to
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
. Another example is given by the class of covariant families, namely
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant if and only if
\widetilde{P}\to B
is right orthogonal to the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

We can also type-theoretically express the
Leibniz construction
familiar from categorical homotopy theory
[
63
, Definitions C.2.8, C.2.10, C.3.8]
as follows. We remark that Leibniz cotensor maps will be ubiquitous in our treatise since the fibrations of interest are defined by conditions on them.

Definition
\thetheorem

(Leibniz cotensor)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion, and
\pi:E\to B
a map between types. The
Leibniz cotensor of
j
and
\pi
(
aka

Leibniz exponential of
\pi
by
j
or
pullback hom
) is defined as the following gap map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pitchfork}}

The map
\pi
is right orthogonal to
j
, meaning that for any square as below there exists a filler uniquely up to homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

if and only if the Leibniz cotensor map is an equivalence

j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi:E^{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y},

cf.
6
.

Though sparsely explicitly present in the text, we will also mention the dual operation.

Definition
\thetheorem

(Pushout product)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
and
k:T\to S
each be type maps or shape inclusions. The
Leibniz tensor of
j
and
k
(or
pushout product
) is defined as the following cogap map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times S\bigsqcup_{Y\times T}X\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\otimes}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\otimes}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}

In particular, recall from
[
61
, Theorem 4.2]
, the explicit formula for the pushout product of two shape inclusions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{t:I\,|\,\varphi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{s:J\,|\,\chi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\langle t,s\rangle:I\times J\,|\,(\varphi\land\zeta)\lor(\psi\land\chi)\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{t:I\,|\,\psi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{s:J\,|\,\zeta\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\langle t,s\rangle:I\times J\,|\,\psi\land\zeta\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}

2.5
Families vs. fibrations

Recall from
[
82
]
that in presence of the univalence axiom, there is an equivalence between type families and fibrations.
9
9

9

Assuming universes with better structural properties—such as the Segal condition or directed univalence—would be fruitful for further considerations, but this is part of future work.

Consider the types

\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}A\to B,\qquad\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{B:\mathcal{U}}(B\to\mathcal{U})

of functions in
\mathcal{U}
(viewed as type-theoretic fibrations
10
10

10

The inhabitants of
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})
are just maps between arbitrary
\mathcal{U}
-small types, but viewed as “
\mathcal{U}
-small type theoretic fibrations over a
\mathcal{U}
-small base”.
), and families with
\mathcal{U}
-small fibers, resp. Both these types naturally are fibered over
\mathcal{U}
via the following maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda A,B,f.B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda B,P.B}

Over a type
B:\mathcal{U}
, we obtain the type of
maps into (or fibrations over)
B
as the fiber:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Theorem 2.1

(Type-theoretic Grothendieck construction, cf.
[
82
, Theorem 4.8.3]
)
.

There is a fiberwise quasi-equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St}

at stage
B:\mathcal{U}
given by a pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B\to\mathcal{U})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}}

with
straightening

\St_{B}(\pi)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\mathrm{fib}_{b}(\pi)

and
unstraightening

\Un_{B}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\widetilde{P},\pi_{P}\rangle

(
\pi_{P}:\widetilde{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b:B}P\,b\to B
the total space projection).

The spirit of dependent type theory somewhat favors type families over fibrations, but we will often resort to the fibrational viewpoint because it allows us to replay familiar categorical arguments. For instance, Riehl–Shulman’s covariant type families are a type-theoretic version of left fibrations, and we want to be able to conveniently make use of both incarnations of the same concept which motivates the following:

Definition 1

(Notions of families and fibrations)
.

A
notion of family (or notion of fibration)
is a family

\mathcal{F}:\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\to\mathrm{Prop}

of propositions on the type of
\mathcal{U}
-small fibrations. For a notion of family
\mathcal{F}
, we say that a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-family
11
11

11

In practice, the name of
\mathcal{F}
will often be a linguistic predicate such as “covariant”, “cocartesian” etc. in which case we drop the hyphen and treat it as part of the natural meta-language, e.g., we will simply speak of “cocartesian” or “covariant fibrations”.
if and only if the proposition

{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathcal{F}(P)

holds. A map
\pi:E\to B
is called an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration
if its family of fibers
\St_{B}(\pi)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-family.

By univalence and the Grothendieck construction, this definition is well-behaved,
i.e.
, a (
\mathcal{U}
-small) map is an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration if and only if it is (equivalent to) a projection associated to an
\mathcal{F}
-family (valued in
\mathcal{U}
).

In particular, we observe the following. Considering

\mathrm{Fib}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,B:\mathcal{U}\\
\pi:E\to B\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\St(\pi)),\quad\mathrm{Fam}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{P:\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(P),

the Grothendieck construction descends to a fiberwise equivalence, for any notion of fibration/family
\mathcal{F}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Remark 2
.

As a convention, we will always state the definitions of the various notions of fibration in terms of
families
, and the above definition schema immediately yields the respective corresponding notion in fibrational terms.

As a convention, we will also often denote a map which satisfies such a fibration condition (or possibly even just a usual map which is to be regarded as a type-theoretic fibration) by a double hooked arrow
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, as is customary in homotopical algebra or categorical homotopy theory.

Furthermore, as a consequence of univalence, any such propositionally defined notion of family/fibration is invariant under equivalence.

Proposition 3

(Homotopy invariance of notions of fibrations)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a notion of fibration. When given a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

the map
\xi
is an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration if and only if
\pi
is.

2.6
Comma and co-/cone types

Definition 4

(Comma types)
.

Consider a cospan of types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The
comma type

f\downarrow g
is given by the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In the case that
f
is the identity
\id_{A}
, we write shorthand
A\mathbin{\downarrow}g
for
f\mathbin{\downarrow}g
, and dually
f\mathbin{\downarrow}A
if
g
is the identity.

Definition 5

(Co-/cone types)
.

Let
X
be a type or a shape and
A
a type. In a setting such as the present one, a map
u:X\to A
is sometimes referred to as an
X
-shaped diagram in
A
. The cospans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}}

give rise to the type
u/A\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}u\mathbin{\downarrow}\mathrm{cst}_{A}
of
cocones in
A
under
u
, and, dually
A/u\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{cst}_{A}\mathbin{\downarrow}u
of
cones in
A
over
u
, resp., defined as comma objects:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(A^{X})^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A/u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(A^{X})^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{1}\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times A^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}\times u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\times\mathrm{cst}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Example 2.2

(Co-/slice types)
.

For a fixed point
b:B
, the
co-/slice types
are defined as
b/B
and
B/b
, resp. Note that
b/B\simeq b\mathbin{\downarrow}B
, and similarly for the slice types.

3
Right orthogonal and LARI families

An important part in our study of synthetic fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories is to provide proofs of certain closure properties, which are chosen to parallel those of
\infty
-cosmoses
[
63
, Definition 1.2.1]
. Recall from
[
63
]
, that any
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}
provides an intrinsic notion of cocartesian fibrations, which themselves form an
\infty
-cosmos
{co\mathcal{C}art}({\mathcal{K}})
. The discrete cocartesian fibrations form an embedded
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{D}iscco\mathcal{C}art({\mathcal{K}})\hookrightarrow{co\mathcal{C}art}({\mathcal{K}})
. We prove type-theoretic analogues of the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties, formulated internally to the type theory of the “ambient”
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial objects.

First, we consider maps which are, more generally, defined by a unique right lifting property against an arbitrary map. Next, we discuss
j
-LARI maps which are defined by a left adjoint right inverse condition on a Leibniz cotensor map.

Specifically, let
j:Y\to X
be some type map.
12
12

12

This is often a shape inclusion; recall from Subsection
2.4
that we have coercion of (strict) shapes into types.
A map
\pi:E\to B
is called
j
-orthogonal
if any square as below has a contractible space of fillers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

If a map
\pi:E\to B
is right orthogonal to a map
j:Y\to X
, we write
j\bot\pi
. Similarly, for families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, we write
j\bot P
if
j\bot\Un_{B}(P)
.

Classes of maps defined by such lifting conditions play an important role in categorical homotopy theory and have been extensively studied in various contexts. In particular, classes defined by right orthogonal lifting conditions necessarily satisfy certain closure properties. We are giving type theoretic proofs which will later apply for the specific kinds of
j
-orthogonal maps that we are interested in, namely (iso)inner fibrations and left fibrations
aka
discrete covariant fibrations. For instance, a map
\pi:E\to B
(over a Segal type
B
) is a covariant fibration if and only if it is right orthogonal to the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

In general,
\pi:E\to B
being
j
-orthogonal can be rephrased as the condition that the gap map in the following diagram be an equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Weakening this condition by requiring the gap map to only have a
left adjoint right inverse (LARI)
leads to the notion of
j
-LARI
map,
i.e.
,
\pi:E\to B
is a
j
-LARI map if and only if the induced map
E^{X}\to B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}
has a LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Between Rezk types, a map
\pi:E\to B
is a cocartesian fibration if and only if it is an
i_{0}
-LARI map, for the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

Both
j
-orthogonal and
j
-LARI maps are closed under dependent products, composition, and pullback. In addition,
j
-orthogonal maps are closed under sequential limits and Leibniz cotensoring. In addition, they satisfy left canceling.

3.1
Right orthogonal fibrations

3.1.1
j
-orthogonal families

Recall from the discussion
[
68
, Section 32.3]
the following formulation of right orthogonality of a map in type theoretic terms.

Proposition 6
.

A map
\pi:E\to B
is right orthogonal to a type map
j:Y\to X
if and only if the
Leibniz cotensor
map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}

is an equivalence,
i.e.
, the following proposition is true:

\prod_{v:X\to B}\prod_{f:\prod_{Y}(j^{*}v)^{*}P}\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:\prod_{X}v^{*}P}j^{*}g=f\Big)

In the case of shape inclusions, by de-/strictifcation we obtain a “strict” version of this statement in terms of extension types (for a special case cf.
[
61
, Theorem 8.5]
):

Corollary 7
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a type family. Then
P
is orthogonal w.r.t. a shape inclusion
t:I\,|\,\varphi\vdash\psi
if and only if

\prod_{v:\Psi\to B}\prod_{f:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(v(t))}\isContr\left(\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(v(t))\middle|^{\Phi}_{f}\right\rangle\right),

where
\Phi
and
\Psi
denote the topes, corresponding to
\varphi
and
\psi
, resp.

At first, we prove the analogous universal properties, for maps between types rather than families. Note that these are equivalent to the constructions given for families since as a general principle of homotopy type theory any map between types can be replaced by the projection of the total space of a family. Likewise, any (homotopy) commutative square can be seen as a map between families.

Stability under pullback follows from an instance of a Pullback Lemma for cube-shaped diagrams as considered by Rijke in
[
68
, Section 24]
.

3.1.2
Closure properties of
j
-orthogonal families

Proposition 8

(Equivalences are
j
-orthogonal)
.

For a type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, any equivalence
f:B^{\prime}\simeq B
is
j
-orthogonal.

Proof 3.1
.

By univalence, we can assume
f
to be the identity
\id_{B}
. Then the claim follows easily.

Proposition 9

(Closedness under dependent products)
.

Let
I
be a type, and
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a family. Assume there is a function
P:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
. If there is a map or shape inclusion
f:Y\to X
such that
f\bot P_{i}
for all
i:I
, we also have
f\bot\prod_{I}P
.

Proof 3.2
.

For
i:I
, denote by
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\to B_{i}
the projection associated to the family
P_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
.
By assumption, the exponential squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

are pullbacks. Since dependent products preserve pullbacks and commute with exponents, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\prod_{i:I}E_{i})^{X}\simeq\prod_{i:I}E_{i}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}^{Y}\simeq(\prod_{i:I}E_{i})^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\prod_{i:I}B_{i})^{X}\simeq\prod_{i:I}B_{i}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}B_{i}^{Y}\simeq(\prod_{i:I}B_{i})^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

as desired.

Corollary 10

(Closedness under binary products)
.

Let
C,B:\mathcal{U}
with families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
. If both
P
and
Q
are orthogonal to a map
j:Y\to X
, then so is the binary product family
P\times Q:B\times C\to\mathcal{U}
.

Corollary 11

(Closedness under exponentiation)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family, orthogonal to a shape inclusion or type map
j:Y\to X
. Then, for any type or shape
Z
, also
P^{Z}
is
j
-orthogonal.

Proposition 12

(Closedness under composition, and left cancelation, cf.
[
63
, Lemma 5.5.9]
)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a map. Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
be families with composite
Q\odot P:B\to\mathcal{U}
. If
j\bot P
then
j\bot Q\odot P
if and only if
j\bot Q
.

Proof 3.3
.

Consider
E\to B
and
F\to B
, the associated total projections to the family
P
and
Q
, resp. Observe that
\widetilde{Q\odot P}\simeq\widetilde{Q}
. We assume
i\bot P
which yields the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then by the Pasting Lemma for pullbacks (e.g.
[
68
, Theorem 22.5.8]
) the composite diagram is a pullback if and only if the upper square is a pullbacks.

In presence of categorical universes of fibrations this statement would imply that the left adjoint to the change of base functor,
i.e.
, postcomposition with the given fibration, preserves and reflects
j
-orthogonality.

Theorem 3.4

(Closedness under pullback along families)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be right orthogonal to a type map
j:Y\to X
. Then, for any pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

the map
\xi\equiv k^{*}\pi:F\to A
is also right orthogonal to
j
.

Proof 3.5
.

By assumption,
E^{X}\simeq B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}
. Now, since exponentiation preserves pullbacks, we obtain the following cube:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We have to show that the back square is a pullback as well, and this follows from the Cube Pullback Lemma
[
68
, Lemma 24.1.4 and Remark 24.1.5]
.

Corollary 13

(Closedness under pullback along maps)
.

Let
B
be a type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a family, and
r:C\to B
a map. Assume
j\bot P
. Then for the pullback
r^{*}P:C\to\mathcal{U}
we have
j\bot r^{*}P
.

Corollary 14

(Fibers of
j
-orthogonal maps)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a
j
-orthogonal family. Then, for any
b:B
the fiber
P\,b
is
j
-orthogonal.

Corollary 15

(Closedness under fiber product)
.

If
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
are
j
-orthogonal families, then so is their fiber product
P\times_{B}Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
.

Corollary 16

(
j
-orthogonal maps from
j
-orthogonal types)
.

Let
B
be a type and
j:Y\to X
a map between types, or a shape inclusion. If the type
B
is
j
-orthogonal, then so is the map
B^{k}:B^{C}\to B^{D}
for all
k:D\to C
.

Corollary 17

(Closedness under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be a
j
-orthogonal map. For any
k:D\to C
, the Leibniz cotensor map
k\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi:E^{C}\to E^{D}\times_{B^{D}}B^{C}
is
j
-orthogonal as well.

Proposition 18

(Orthogonal families are closed under sequential limits)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map. Consider a tower of maps given by
A:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{U}
together with
f:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n+1}\to A_{n}
such that for any
n:\mathbb{N}
, the map
f_{n}:A_{n+1}\to A_{n}
is
j
-orthogonal. Then, for the sequential limit
A_{\infty}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lim_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lim_{n:\mathbb{N}}\langle A_{n},f_{n}\rangle
, the map
\pi_{0}:A_{\infty}\to A_{0}
is
j
-orthogonal as well.
13
13

13

Then, by left cancelation so are all projections
\pi_{n}:A_{\infty}\to A_{n}
for
n:\mathbb{N}
.

Proof 3.6
.

By precondition, for any
n:\mathbb{N}
we have equivalences
A_{n+1}^{X}\simeq A_{n}^{X}\times_{A_{n}^{Y}}A_{n+1}^{Y}
via the structure maps. By composition, we get equivalences
A_{n}^{X}\simeq A_{0}^{X}\times_{A_{0}^{Y}}A_{n}^{Y}
. In sum, we obtain

A_{\infty}^{X}\simeq\lim_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}^{X}\simeq\lim_{n:\mathbb{N}}\big(A_{0}^{X}\times_{A_{0}^{Y}}A_{n}^{Y}\big)\simeq A_{0}^{X}\times_{A_{0}^{Y}}A_{\infty}^{Y},

again via the structure maps.

Proposition 19

(Maps between
j
-orthogonal types are
j
-orthogonal)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. If both
E
and
B
are
j
-orthogonal types, then any map
\pi:E\to B
is
j
-orthogonal as well.

Proof 3.7
.

Since
B
is
j
-orthogonal, the restriction map
B^{X}\to B^{Y}
is an equivalence, and so is its pullback along
E^{Y}\to B^{Y}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now, since by assumption
E^{X}\to E^{Y}
is an equivalence is well, so is the gap map by
2
-for-
3
for equivalences.

Proposition 20

(Total types of
j
-orthogonal maps)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion and
\pi:E\to B
be a map with
j
-orthogonal codomain. Then
E
is
j
-orthogonal if and only if
\pi
is.

Proof 3.8
.

Similar as for
19
.

3.2
LARI families

For a type map
j:Y\to X
, we call a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a
j
-LARI family
if the Leibniz cotensor map
j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi:\widetilde{P}^{X}\to\widetilde{P}^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}
has a left adjoint right inverse (LARI). Typically,
j:Y\to X
will be a shape inclusion. In fact, we eventually consider the case of the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
which precisely defines the notion of cocartesian fibration.

However, already for general maps
j:Y\to X
we can prove that the
j
-LARI fibrations satisfy several closure properties considered before in the case of
j
-orthogonal fibrations.

A basic account of LARI adjunctions in the synthetic setting is given in Appendix
B
, based on Riehl–Shulman’s theory of adjunctions
[
61
, Section 11]
.

Remark 21
.

We also note the following. If
U:E\to B:F
is a LARI adjunction of Segal types,
we may assume—by
fibrant replacement
, cf.
[
82
, Theorem 4.8.3]
and
Section

2.5
—that
E
is the total type of a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
that
U
is the projection, and that
F\,x\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle x,f\,x\rangle

for some section
f:\prod_{x:B}P\,x
.

In any LARI adjuction, the left adjoint is fully faithful,
as
\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{B}(b,UF\,b^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{E}(F\,b,F\,b^{\prime})
.

3.2.1
LARI families

Definition 22

(LARI families)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family, and
j:Y\to X
be any type map or shape inclusion. Writing
\pi:E\to B
for the associated fibration, we call
P
a
j
-LARI family
if and only we have a LARI adjunction (in one of the equivalent formulations of
Theorem

B.1
) in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

LARI fibrations generalize right orthogonal fibrations, as we will see.

Proposition 23

(Adjoint equivalences,
[
63
, Proposition 2.1.11/12]
)
.

Let
u:B\to A
be an equivalence between Rezk types. Then there exists a functor
f:A\to B
s.t.
f\dashv u
.

Here,
f
can be taken to be a quasi-inverse of
u
.

Proof 3.9
.

Since
u:B\to A
is an equivalence it has a quasi-inverse,
i.e.
, there exists a map
f:A\to B
together with homotopies
\eta:\id_{A}=uf
,
\varepsilon:fu=\id_{B}
. Then, by
2
-for-
3
for isomorphisms in a Rezk type,
14
14

14

The
2
-for-
3
law for isomorphisms in a Segal type can easily be proven as in the case of
1
-categories, using
[
61
, Proposition 10.1]
.
this gives homotopies
u\varepsilon\circ\eta u=\id_{u}
and
\varepsilon f\circ f\eta=\id_{f}
. Together, these data make up a quasi-diagrammatic adjunction which can be promoted to a coherent, bi-diagrammatic adjunction by
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
.

Corollary 24
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a
j
-orthogonal family over a Rezk type
B
. Then
P
is also a
j
-LARI family.

3.2.2
Closure properties of
j
-LARI families

Proposition 25

(Closedness under products)
.

Let
I
be a type, and
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a family. Assume there is a function
P:\prod_{i:I}B(i)\to\mathcal{U}
. If there is a map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
such that
P(i)
is a
j
-LARI family for each
i:I
, then also
\prod_{I}P
is a
j
-LARI family

Proof 3.10
.

Let
E
the total type of
P
and
E_{i}
the total type of
P_{i}
, for any
i:I
. Observe that we have

\displaystyle\left(X\to\prod_{I}E\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\sigma:X\to\prod_{I}B}\prod_{i:I}\left(\prod_{x:X}P_{i}((\sigma\,x)_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\sigma:X\to\prod_{I}B}\left(X\to\prod_{I}P\right)(\sigma).

For any
i:I
, we are given a LARI adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}^{X}\times_{B_{i}^{Y}}E_{i}^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

which induces a LARI adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\prod_{I}E)^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\prod_{I}B)^{X}\times_{(\prod_{I}B)^{Y}}(\prod_{I}E)^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

by
121
, since exponentiation commutes with dependent products.

Corollary 26

(Closedness of
j
-LARI families under binary products)
.

Let
C,B:\mathcal{U}
with families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
. If both
P
and
Q
are
j
-LARI families w.r.t. to a map
j:Y\to X
, then so is the binary product family
P\times Q:B\times C\to\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 27

(Closedness of
j
-LARI families under composition)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a map. Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
be families with composite
Q\odot P:B\to\mathcal{U}
. If both
P
and
Q
are
j
-LARI families, then so is
Q\odot P
.

Proof 3.11
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
and
\xi:F\to E
be the projection maps associated to
P
and
Q
, respectively. Abbreviating
S:\equiv B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}
,
T:\equiv E^{X}\times_{E^{Y}}F^{Y}
and
R\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}F^{Y}
, from the LARI adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\vdash

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\vdash

we want to get a LARI adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime\prime}}
\vdash

Factoring the pullback square for
T
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\vdash
\vdash
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\vdash

yields the claim, by first pulling back the LARI adjunction between
E^{X}
and
S
, and then composing with the one between
F^{X}
and
T
. Indeed, by
123
LARI adjunctions compose.

Theorem 3.12

(Closedness of
j
-LARI families under pullback)
.

Let
B
be a type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family, and
k:A\to B
be a map. Given a map
j:Y\to X
, if
P
is a
j
-LARI family, then the pullback
k^{*}P:A\to\mathcal{U}
is a
j
-LARI family as well.

Proof 3.13
.

By assumption, writing
\pi:E\to B
and
\xi:F\to A
for the associated projection maps, we have a pullback together with a LARI adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

We have to show that the map
j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\xi:F^{X}\to F^{Y}\times_{A^{Y}}A^{X}
has a LARI. Note first that we can factor it through equivalences as follows:

\displaystyle{F^{X}\simeq A^{X}\times_{B^{X}}E^{X}}\longrightarrow{A^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}\simeq A^{X}\times_{A^{Y}}(A^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y})\simeq A^{X}\times_{A^{Y}}F^{Y}}

(3.1)

Next, observe that by the Pullback Lemma, the inner left square is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Similarly, the top square becomes a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times_{B^{X}}E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}A^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdash}

LARI adjunctions are closed under pullback by
122
. Hence, this serves to induce the LARI of the map

A^{X}\times_{B^{X}}E^{X}\to E^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}A^{X}

which corresponds to
j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\xi:F^{X}\to F^{Y}\times_{A^{Y}}A^{X}
by
3.1
.

Corollary 28

(Closedness of
j
-LARI families under pullback along families)
.

Let
B
be a types, and
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
be families. If
P
is a
j
-LARI family, then the pullback family
Q^{*}P:\widetilde{Q}\to\mathcal{U}
is as well.

Corollary 29

(Closedness of
j
-LARI families under fiber product)
.

If
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
are
j
-LARI families, then so is the fiber product
P\times_{B}Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
.

4
(Iso)inner families

Our main objects of study are cocartesian families,
i.e.
, functorial families of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories.

Since at the most general level types are not Segal, as an intermediate step to defining cocartesian families, we have to deal with families of (complete) Segal types that are not necessarily functorial.
Inner families
are those type families for which the associated projection is right orthogonal to the horn inclusion
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
. Hence, between Segal types, inner families correspond to fibrations in the Segal model structure. Bringing in Rezk completeness motivates our definition of
isoinner family
, which in addition to being inner requires all fibers to be Rezk-complete. Over Rezk types, this can be expressed by requiring the associated projection to be right orthogonal to the terminal projection from the free bi-invertible arrow
\mathbb{E}
.

In this section, we discuss the behavior of these non-functorial families of Segal and Rezk types, respectively.
Several closure properties will follow from the results of
Section

3
. We also introduce the free bi-invertible arrow
\mathbb{E}
, constructed as a colimit after
[
61
, Appendix A.3]
.
15
15

15

The walking bi-invertible arrow hence arises as a
type
in our case, although it might be possible to obtain it as a shape by a suitable extension of the shape theory.
Along the way, we characterize Rezk types via right orthogonality w.r.t. either inclusion
\mathbf{1}\to\mathbb{E}
, or alternatively, the terminal projection
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
.

4.1
Inner families

We introduce the notion of an inner family, resembling—in some sense—
inner
or
mid fibrations
from quasi-category theory, used by Joyal and Lurie as an auxiliary tool to investigate co-/cartesian fibrations.

In the same vein, an inner family can be seen as a
relativized
version of the notion of Segal type: in an inner family any
(2,1)
-horn sitting over a
2
-simplex in the base can be extended, uniquely up to homotopy, to a
2
-simplex lying over. In particular, an inner family over the terminal type
\mathbf{1}
is the same as a Segal type.

4.1.1
Definition and characterization

Definition 30

(Inner Family)
.

Let
B
be a type. A family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is called
inner family
if

{\mathrm{isInnerFam}}(P):\equiv\prod_{\sigma:\Delta^{2}\to B}\prod_{\eta:\prod_{t:\Lambda_{1}^{2}}P(\sigma(t))}\isContr\left(\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}P\,\sigma(t)\middle|^{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}_{\eta}\right\rangle\right).

Unpacking this gives the following logically equivalent proposition:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{isInnerFam}}(P)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\prod_{b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:b\to b^{\prime}\\
v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}\\
w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\sigma:u,v\Rightarrow_{B}w}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e:Pb\\
e^{\prime}:Pb^{\prime}\\
e^{\prime\prime}:Pb^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:e\to_{u}^{P}e^{\prime}\\
g:e^{\prime}\to^{P}_{v}e^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\isContr\left(\sum_{h:e\to^{P}_{w}e^{\prime\prime}}f,g\Rightarrow_{w}^{P}h\right).

Figure 5:
In an inner family, inner
2
-horns can be extended uniquely up to homotopy relative to a given
2
-simplex in the base.

This can be expressed in categorical terms as the condition that the unstraightening
\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B
be right orthogonal to
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
, or equivalently, the Leibniz cotensor of these maps be an equivalence.

Proposition 31

(Orthogonality characterization of inner families)
.

A type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is inner if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Delta^{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}}

is a homotopy pullback.

Proof 4.1
.

This is an instance of
7
.

4.1.2
Closure and structural properties

As an instance of
Section

3.1.2
it follows that inner families enjoy several closure properties.

Proposition 32
.

Inner families over arbitrary types are closed under fibered equivalences, dependent products, composition, pullback, left cancelation, and sequential limits. Families corresponding to equivalences are always isoinner.

Proposition 33
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family. Then for every
b:B
the fiber
P\,b
is a Segal type.

Proof 4.2
.

This is an instance of
14
.

Proposition 34
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family over a Segal type
B
. Then the total type
\widetilde{P}:\equiv\sum_{b:B}P\,b
is Segal if and only if
P
is an inner family.

Proof 4.3
.

This is an instance of
20
.

The following observation reflects the semantic fact that between Segal spaces Reedy fibrations coincide with the fibrations of the Segal space model structure. It is also a formal consequence of
19
.

Proposition 35

(Maps between Segal types are inner)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be a map between Segal types
B,E
. Then
\pi:E\to B
is inner.

4.2
Isoinner families

As a next step towards the synthetic version of cocartesian fibrations, we consider non-functorial families of
complete
Segal,
aka
Rezk types. To be able to formulate an appropriate right orthogonality condition, we start by defining the
free
(or
walking
)
bi-invertible arrow
. This gives alternative formulations of the Rezk-completeness condition.

4.2.1
The free bi-invertible arrow

In order to exhibit Rezk completeness as an orthogonality condition, we want to define a type
\mathbb{E}
which for any type corepresents its type of isomorphisms,
i.e.
,

{\mathrm{iso}}(X)\simeq(\mathbb{E}\to X)

for any type
X:\mathcal{U}
. Moreover, this will also allow us to define a notion of (non-functorial) family of Rezk types, which we call
isoinner families
. Semantically, these correspond—at least between fibrant objects—to the fibrations of the Rezk model structure.

We internalize the construction of
\mathbb{E}
due to
[
61
, Definition A. 24,25]
, by forming the colimit
\mathbb{E}
of the following diagram
\mathcal{D}
of types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}

where
d^{k}:\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{2}
is the
k
-th face inclusion,
i.e.
, the “injection whose image is missing
k
”. Concretely, this can be done using the theory of colimits over reflexive graphs as described by Rijke
[
67
, Section 3]
.

The free bi-invertible arrow is constructed as a colimit over a reflexive graph.
Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the graph underlying the diagram
\mathcal{D}
of types as shown above. By
[
67
, Remark 3.5.6]
, it follows that the colimit of the
\mathcal{G}
-diagram
\mathcal{D}
is given by the pushout of the cospan
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D}):\equiv\langle\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\rangle
associated to
\mathcal{D}
via the total graph:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle i,x\rangle,\\
\langle j,y\rangle:\widetilde{D_{0}}\end{subarray}}G_{1}(i,j)\times D_{1}(x)=y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{D_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{D_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proposition 36
.

The colimit
\mathbb{E}
of the diagram
\mathcal{D}
as above (covariantly) represents the types of isomorphisms in a Segal type,
i.e.
, for any Segal type
A
there is an equivalence of types

(\mathbb{E}\to A)\simeq{\mathrm{iso}}(A).

Proof 4.4
.

We leave out the explicit descriptions of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{D}
, but they are straightforward. By the universal property of the pushout, for an arbitrary type
A:\mathcal{U}
, there is an equivalence

(\mathbb{E}\to A)\simeq\cocone_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D})}(A).

Then it follows by unwinding the data of the diagram
\mathcal{D}
that there is a chain of equivalences

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}\to A)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{a,b:A}\sum_{\sigma,\tau:\Delta^{2}\to A}\left(\sigma\circ d^{1}=\id_{b}\right)\times\left(\sigma\circ d^{2}=\tau\circ d^{0}\right)\times\left(\tau\circ d^{1}=\id_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{a,b:A}\sum_{\sigma,\tau:\Delta^{2}\to A}\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}\sigma(t,t)=\id_{b}\times\sigma(1,t)=\tau(t,0)\times\tau(t,t)=\id_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{a,b:A}\sum_{f:a\to b}\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to A\middle|^{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}_{[f,\id_{b}]}\right\rangle\times\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to A\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[f,\id_{a}]}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{a,b:A}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:a\to b\\
g,h:b\to a\end{subarray}}fg=\id_{b}\times fh=\id_{a}

(
A
is Segal, cf.
[
61
, Proposition 5.10]
)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathrm{iso}}(A),

where one invokes de-/strictifcation as necessary.

4.2.2
Rezk types

For the walking bi-invertible arrow
\mathbb{E}
, consider the terminal projection
!_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
and the inclusion maps
j_{k}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{E}
for
k=0,1
. For a fixed type
B
, we write
\partial_{k}:\equiv B^{j_{k}}:B^{\mathbb{E}}\to B
. We prove a characterization of Rezk types à la
[
58
, Proposition 6.4]
.
In particular, the Rezk types are exactly those among Segal types that are
\mathbb{E}
-null. This is in line with
[
58
, Theorem 6.2]
.

In general, given a map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
we say a type
B
is
j
-local
(cf.
[
69
, Definition 2.1]
) if the terminal projection
!_{B}:B\to\mathbf{1}
is
j
-orthogonal which is equivalent to the map
B^{j}:B^{X}\to B^{Y}
being an equivalence.
E.g.
, a type is Segal iff it is
\iota
-local, where
\iota:\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
.

In case
j\equiv!_{Y}:Y\to\mathbf{1}
is a terminal projection and
B
is
!_{Y}
-local we say that
B
is
Y
-null
.

Proposition 37

(Characterization of Rezk types)
.

Let
B
be a Segal type. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

The type
B
is Rezk,
i.e.
,

\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B})\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y:B\\
f:{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(x,y)\end{subarray}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v:B\\
p:(u=_{B}v)\end{subarray}}\langle u,v,{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B,u,v}(p)\rangle=\langle x,y,f\rangle\Big).

2.

The type
B
is
\mathbb{E}
-null, meaning
B
is
!_{\mathbb{E}}
-local,
i.e.
,

\isEquiv(B^{!_{\mathbb{E}}})\simeq\prod_{f:B^{\mathbb{E}}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{x:B}f=\id_{x}\Big).

3.

The type
B
is
j_{k}
-local for
k=0
or
k=1
,
i.e.
,

\isEquiv(B^{j_{k}})\simeq\prod_{x:B}\isContr\Big(\sum_{f:B^{\mathbb{E}}}\partial_{k}f=x\Big).

Proof 4.5
.

\ref{thm:rezk-idtoiso-equiv}\implies\ref{thm:rezk-j-local}
:

By assumption, for
\langle x,y,f\rangle:\sum_{x,y:B}{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(x,y)
there exists, uniquely up to homotopy, a datum
\langle u,v,p\rangle:\sum_{u,v:B}(u=_{B}v)
such that
\langle u,v,p\rangle=\langle x,y,{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B,u,v}(p)\rangle
. By path induction, we can take
\langle u,v,p\rangle:\equiv\langle x,x,{\mathrm{refl}}_{x}\rangle
, thus yielding
x:B
uniquely with
