f=\id_{x}
.

\ref{thm:rezk-j-local}\implies\ref{thm:rezk-idtoiso-equiv}
:

By assumption, it suffices to find for any
x:B
, uniquely up to homotopy a path
p:(u=v)
,
u,v:B
with
{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B,u,v}(p)=\id_{x}
. By path induction,
p
can be taken to be
{\mathrm{refl}}_{x}
.

\ref{thm:rezk-j-local}\implies\ref{thm:rezk-i-local}
:

For any
x:B
clearly
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{x}
is an isomorphism with
\partial_{k}(f)=x
. By assumption however, for
any
isomorphism
f:B^{\mathbb{E}}
, there is a path from
f
to some identity.

\ref{thm:rezk-i-local}\implies\ref{thm:rezk-j-local}
:

Let
f:{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(x,y)
,
x,y:B
. By assumption, any
g:B^{\mathbb{E}}
with
\partial_{k}(g)=x
is uniquely determined up to homotopy. Thus,
f=\id_{x}
.

NB

Even though the
!_{\mathbb{E}}
-local and the
j_{k}
-local Segal types coincide, the relative version of this statement is not true: not every
!_{\mathbb{E}}
-orthogonal family or map is
j_{k}
-orthogonal. As a counterexample, consider
\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\to\mathbb{E}
.

4.2.3
Isoinner families

We introduce the notion of isoinner family over general bases, even though we mostly will require the base to be (complete) Segal. Over Segal bases, being isoinner can be expressed through relative Segal and Rezk conditions. Then it follows again by
Section

3.1.2
that these families are closed under several operations.

Definition 38

(Isoinner family)
.

A type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is an
isoinner family
if it is an inner family and every fiber is
!_{\mathbb{E}}
-local,
i.e.
, the proposition

{\mathrm{isIsoInnerFam}}(P):\equiv{\mathrm{isInnerFam}}(P)\times\prod_{b:B}\prod_{f:{\mathrm{iso}}(P\,b)}\isContr\Big(\sum_{e:P\,b}f=\id_{e}\Big)

is inhabited.

In case
B
is a Segal type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
being an isoinner family is equivalent to

{\mathrm{isIsoInnerFam}}(P)\,\simeq{\mathrm{isInnerFam}}(P)\times\prod_{b:B}\isRezk(P\,b)

by
37
.

Clearly, an isoinner family over
\mathbf{1}
is the same as a Rezk type.

Recall in particular, that total spaces of inner families over Segal types are Segal. It turns out that over Segal types, being isoinner can be expressed as a right orthogonality property.

Proposition 39

(Orthogonality characterization of isoinner families)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a family s.t. every fiber
P\,b
is Segal. Then every fiber is Rezk if and only if
\pi:E\to B
is right orthogonal to
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
,
i.e.
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\mathbb{E}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\mathbb{E}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{pttoid}}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\total({\mathrm{pttoid}}_{P})}

is a homotopy pullback, where

{\mathrm{pttoid}}_{B}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\id_{b},\quad{\mathrm{pttoid}}_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,e.\langle\id_{b},\id_{e}\rangle.

Proof 4.6
.

The diagram is a pullback if and only if

\prod_{b:B}\prod_{f:\mathbb{E}\to P\,b}\isContr\big(\sum_{e:P\,b}\id_{e}=_{\mathbb{E}\to P\,b}f\big).

By
37
this is equivalent to every fiber being Rezk.

Corollary 40
.

For any type
B
, an inner family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is isoinner if and only if it is right orthogonal to
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
.

Proposition 41
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family. Then the total type
\widetilde{P}:\equiv\sum_{b:B}P\,b
is a Rezk type.

Proof 4.7
.

This is again an instance of
20
.

Formally from Subsection
3.1.2
it follows that, over Segal bases, isoinner families enjoy several closure properties.

Proposition 42
.

Isoinner families over Segal types are closed under dependent products, composition, pullback, left cancelation, and sequential limits. Families corresponding to equivalences are always isoinner.

5
Cocartesian families

Cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
encode copresheaves of
(\infty,1)
-categories. All fibers
P\,b
are Rezk types, and
P
is (covariantly)
functorial
in the sense that an arrow
u:a\to b
in
B
induces a functor
u_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b
, and this transport operation is natural w.r.t. directed arrows in
B
,
i.e.
, it respects composition and identities. In fact, we will often reason about cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
in terms of their associated projection
\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi_{P}:E\to B
. Our study is informed by
[
63
, Chapter 5]
and
[
64
]
in an essential way, where Riehl–Verity develop a model-independent theory of cocartesian fibrations intrinsic to an arbitrary
\infty
-cosmos. While this constitutes more generally a fibrational theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories, for
0\leq n\leq\infty
, our present study restricts to
(\infty,1)
-categories, and at the same time extends Riehl–Shulman’s treatment of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories fibered in
\infty
-groupoids.

Reminiscent to the classical (1-categorical) definition, we introduce cocartesian families in terms of the existence of enough cocartesian liftings. However, we also give alternative characterizations, such as the
Chevalley criterion
which allows us to develop the theory in the style of formal category theory, as done by Riehl and Verity
[
63
]
for
\infty
-cosmoses.

Specifically, over Rezk types cocartesian families are exactly the isoinner families that are
i_{0}
-LARI families in the sense of Subsection
3.2
, for
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
. Spelled out, this means that the gap map in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

has a left adjoint right inverse
\chi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E^{\Delta^{1}}
which yields the up to homotopy uniquely determined cocartesian lifts.

We proceed by studying
cocartesian functors
between cocartesian families (incarnated as fibrations). Then, using Subsection
3.2.2
, we are able to prove type-theoretic versions of the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties of cocartesian fibrations, which in our case means that the
(\infty,1)
-category of cocartesian fibrations is complete w.r.t. to certain
(\infty,1)
-limits.
16
16

16

In more technical terms, our results can be interpreted as type-theoretic proofs of the completeness of the
(\infty,1)
-categorical core of the
\infty
-cosmos
co\mathcal{C}art(\mathcal{R}ezk)
, itself presenting an
(\infty,2)
-category (cf.
[
63
, Definition 12.1.10, Remark 12.1.11]
).
Note that, ideally, these would be statements involving universe types which themselves are Rezk. These are beyond the scope of the current discussion, but nevertheless we can “externalize” these completeness statements to our univalent universe
\mathcal{U}
of arbitrary simplicial types, yielding
90
.

Finally, we prove characterizations of cocartesian functors complementing the ones for cocartesian fibrations, e.g., the Chevalley criterion for cocartesian functors says that a fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

between cocartesian fibrations is a cocartesian functor if and only if the mate of the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

is invertible.
17
17

17

A development of the required results about adjunctions in simplicial type theory is given in
Sections

A.1
and
A.2
.
As an application, we prove that fibered adjunctions between cocartesian fibrations are cocartesian functors, adapting Riehl–Verity’s proof in
\infty
-cosmoses to the type-theoretic setting.

5.1
Cocartesian arrows

Just as in
1
-category theory, there is also a notion of cocartesian arrow in higher dimensional category theory. These are dependent arrows
18
18

18

In fact, in general
\infty
-cosmoses Riehl–Verity consider cocartesian
2
-cells between generalized elements
[
63
, Definition 5.1.1]
. In simplicial type theory one could imagine a similar development as well, using exponential transposition and closedness of cocartesian families under dependent products, cf. also
[
63
, Lemma 5.6.5]
.
satisfying a certain initiality property.

In this section, we provide a few characterizations, analogous to Joyal and Lurie’s for quasi-categories, and a few properties that will be useful later on when investigating cocartesian fibrations.

5.1.1
Definition and basic properties

Definition 43

(Cocartesian arrow)
.

Let
B
be a type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family.
Let
b,b^{\prime}:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
, and
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
. An arrow
f:\hom^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
is a
(
P
-)cocartesian morphism
or
(
P
-)cocartesian arrow
if and only if

{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}f:\equiv\prod_{\sigma:\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Delta_{0}^{1}}_{u}\right\rangle}\prod_{h:\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P\,\sigma(t,t)}\isContr\left(\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P\sigma(t,s)\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[f,h]}\right\rangle\right).

This is illustrated in
Figure

6
. Notice that being a cocartesian arrow is a proposition.

Figure 6:
The universal property of cocartesian arrows

By expressing the functions on simplices in terms of
objects, morphisms and composition, we obtain an equivalent type:

\begin{split}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}f\simeq&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b^{\prime\prime}:B\\
v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}\\
w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\sigma:u,v\Rightarrow_{B}w}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}\\
h:e\to^{P}_{w}e^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:e^{\prime}\to_{v}^{P}e^{\prime\prime}}(f,g\Rightarrow_{\sigma}^{P}h)\Big)\end{split}

(5.1)

Diagrammatically, this is expressed as the existence of a filler, unique up to homotopy, as in a diagram of the following form, cf.
Section

2.4
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

NB

We will refrain from reasoning about cocartesian arrows at this level of generality,
i.e.
, for the case of arbitrary type families with non-trivial composition
2
-cells. Hence, from now on we will mostly consider families of (complete) Segal types.

Notation:
Let
f
be a cocartesian arrow. Then, given an arrow
v
in the base with
\partial_{1}(\pi\,f)=\partial_{0}(v)
and
h
a dependent arrow over
v\circ\pi(f)
with
\partial_{0}\,f=\partial_{0}\,h
, we denote by
\mathrm{fill}_{v,f}(h)
the arrow
g
determined uniquely up to homotopy such that
gf=h
. In the notation, we will usually leave the arrow
v
implicit, writing
\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h)
. This is justified syntactically since syntactically the datum
v
can be inferred from
f
. But please do keep in my mind that the universal property of
f
quantifies explicitly first over
v
, then
h
.

Definition 44

(Cocartesian lift)
.

Let
B
be a type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family.
For
b,b^{\prime}:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
, and
e:P\,b
,
we define the type of
(
P
-)cocartesian lifts of
u

starting at
e
to be

{\mathrm{CocartLift}}_{P}(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}}\sum_{f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}f.

For Segal types, where composites are uniquely determined,
we can further rewrite (
5.1
):
19
19

19

Here, composition in the family is to be understood as

dependent composition
in the sense of
[
61
, Remark 8.11]
, though we will mostly leave this implicit in our notation.

{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}f\simeq\prod_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\penalty\ \prod_{v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}b^{\prime\prime}}\penalty\ \prod_{e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}}\penalty\ \prod_{h:e\to^{P}_{v\circ u}e^{\prime\prime}}\isContr\Bigl(\sum_{g:e^{\prime}\to^{P}_{v}e^{\prime\prime}}g\circ^{P}f=h\Bigr).

In fact, we recognize the right-hand side as expressing the initiality
of the object
\langle b^{\prime},\id_{b^{\prime}},e^{\prime},f\rangle
in the type

A(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\penalty\ \sum_{v:b^{\prime}\to_{B}b^{\prime\prime}}\penalty\ \sum_{e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}}(e\to^{P}_{v\circ u}e^{\prime\prime}).

(5.2)

Indeed,
A(u,e)
is a Segal type as can be seen as follows.

First, note that we can use the types

b^{*}B^{\Delta^{2}}\simeq\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Delta_{0}^{0}}_{b}\right\rangle,\quad b^{*}E^{\Delta^{1}}\simeq\sum_{u:b\downarrow B}\sum_{e:P\,b}e\downarrow E

as strict models for the pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{*}B^{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{*}E^{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

These serve to define the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{*}E^{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{*}B^{\Delta^{2}}\times P\,b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\downarrow B\times P\,b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\times\id_{P\,b}}

where

q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda w,d,d^{\prime},f.\langle w,d\rangle:b^{*}E^{\Delta^{1}}\to b\downarrow B\times P\,b

and

r\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,v.v\circ u:b^{*}B^{\Delta^{2}}\to b\downarrow B.

All the occuring types are Segal, hence
S\to b\downarrow B\times P\,b
is an inner fibration, equivalent to the projection
\widetilde{A}\simeq\sum_{\langle u,e\rangle:b\downarrow B\times P\,b}A(u,e)\to b\downarrow B\times P\,b
.

In particular
\widetilde{A}\simeq S
is Segal. Initiality in the type
A(u,e)
implies furthermore that cocartesian lifts (w.r.t. the given data) are uniquely determined up to homotopy.

Proposition 45

(Uniqueness of cocartesian lifts (in isoinner families),
[
63
, Lemma 5.1.11]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Then
P
-cocartesian lifts of arrows of
B
are unique up to homotopy.

Corollary 46
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family over a Rezk type
B
.

1.

If
f:e\to e^{\prime}
is a
P
-cocartesian arrow, and
h:e^{\prime}\to e^{\prime\prime}
is an arbitrary arrow, then
\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h)\circ f=h
.

2.

If
f:e\to e^{\prime}
is a
P
-cocartesian arrow, and
g:e^{\prime}\to e^{\prime\prime}
is an arbitrary arrow, then
\mathrm{fill}_{f}(gf)=g
.

As an indication that our definition of cocartesian arrow is correct,
we note the following expected result.

Proposition 47
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and let
b^{\prime}\xleftarrow{u}{}b\xrightarrow{v}{}c

be a span in
B
.
This span is equivalently determined by an arrow
u
in
B

and an element
v
in the domain fibration
\partial_{0}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B

over
b
, the domain of
u
.
Then a cocone
\langle v^{\prime},f,\alpha\rangle
of the span, giving a square in
B
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

makes a
pushout square
, if and only if
\langle f,\alpha\rangle
is a cocartesian
lift of
u
starting at
v
.

Let us first clarify what we mean by a pushout (and, for the sake of completeness, a pullback) in a Rezk type. Recall from
Section

2.6
the definition of cocone types.

Definition 48

(Co-/span shape)
.

The
span
and
cospan shape
, resp., are defined as the shapes

\ulcorner\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}^{2}\;|\;t\equiv 0\lor s\equiv 0\},\quad\lrcorner\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}^{2}\;|\;t\equiv 1\lor s\equiv 1\}.

Of course there are weak equivalences

\ulcorner\simeq\Lambda_{0}^{2},\quad\lrcorner\simeq\Lambda_{2}^{2},

but these pairs of shapes do not coincide on the strict level of topes.

Definition 49

(Pullbacks and pushouts in a Rezk type)
.

A
span

\sigma
in a Rezk type is a diagram
\sigma:B^{\ulcorner}
. Dually, a cospan
\tau
is a diagram
\tau:B^{\lrcorner}
. A
pullback over
\sigma
in
B
is a terminal element in the cone type
B/\sigma
. Dually, a
pushout over
\tau
in
B
is an initial element in the cocone type
\sigma/B
.

The type of cocones can be rewritten as

\sigma/B\simeq\left\langle\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\to B\middle|^{\ulcorner}_{\sigma}\right\rangle.

For a span
\sigma:B^{\ulcorner}
with
\sigma\equiv(c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\leftarrow}}a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\to}}b)
, a cocone
\vartheta:\sigma/B
is given by a span
\vartheta\equiv(c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\leftarrow}}b)
.

One checks that a cocone
\vartheta\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle x,\iota_{0},\iota_{1}\rangle:\sigma/B
is a pushout square if and only if it satisfies the familiar universal property
20
20

20

It is possible to provide a completely strict description replacing the identity types by strict extensions. This could be done elegantly using a join operation on shapes, as is common in simplicial homotopy theory, but we will not need this here.
internally to the given Rezk type:

{\mathrm{isPushout}}_{B,\sigma}(\vartheta)\simeq\prod_{\kappa\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\leftarrow}}b):\sigma/B}\isContr\Big(\sum_{\gamma:x\to d}\gamma\circ\iota_{0}=f\times\gamma\circ\iota_{1}=g\Big)

One can make similar considerations for pullbacks internal to a Rezk type.

Proof 5.1

(Proof of
47
)
.

We observed above that
\langle f,\alpha\rangle
is a cocartesian lift of
u

starting at
v
, if and only if it is an initial object of the
type (
5.2
), which in the case of the
domain family
P(b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{c:B}(b\to_{B}c)
becomes

A(u,v)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\penalty\ \sum_{u^{\prime}:b^{\prime}\to_{B}b^{\prime\prime}}\penalty\ \sum_{\langle c^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle:P\,b^{\prime}}\penalty\ \bigl((c,v)\to^{P}_{u^{\prime}\circ u}(c^{\prime},v^{\prime})\bigr),

as illustrated below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}\circ u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}

There is a forgetful map
U
from this type to the type of cocones,
and
U
has a LARI given by taking
u^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{b^{\prime}}
.
Now we conclude by
124
.

Proposition 50

(Closedness under composition, and right cancelation,
[
63
, Lemma 5.1.5]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family over a Rezk type
B
. For arrows
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, with
b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B
, consider dependent arrows
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
,
g:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
, for
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
,
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}
.

1.

If both
f
and
g
are are cocartesian arrows, then so is their composite
g\circ f
.

2.

If
f
and
g\circ f
are cocartesian arrows, then so is
g
.

Proof 5.2
.

1.

Let
w:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
b^{\prime\prime\prime}:B
. Writing
r\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}w\circ v\circ u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime\prime})
, let
h:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{r}(e,e^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
e^{\prime\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Define
m\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{g}(\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h)):{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{w}(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime})
, then
m\circ(g\circ f)=h
.

For any
m^{\prime}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{w}(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
h=m^{\prime}\circ(g\circ f)=(m^{\prime}\circ g)\circ f
, necessarily
m^{\prime}\circ g=\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h)
. But then
m^{\prime}=\mathrm{fill}_{g}(\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h))
. Thus,
g\circ f
is cocartesian.

2.

Let
w\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v\circ u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})
and
h\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}g\circ h:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{w}(e,e^{\prime\prime})
. For
b^{\prime\prime\prime}:B
, consider
r:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime})
and write
t\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}r\circ v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime})
. Let
k:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{t}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
e^{\prime\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Since
h=gf
is cocartesian, there is a filler
m:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{r}(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
m=\mathrm{fill}_{h}(kf)
, so
mh=kf
.

Then in turn
k=\mathrm{fill}_{f}(mh)
. But since
kf=(mg)f
, we have
k=mg
.

Given any
m^{\prime}
s.t.
m^{\prime}h=kf
, this means
m^{\prime}=\mathrm{fill}_{h}(kf)=m
.

Lemma 51

(
[
63
, Lemma 5.1.6]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family over a Segal type
B
.

1.

If
f
is a dependent isomorphism in
P
over some morphism
u
in
B
, then
u
is itself an isomorphism.

2.

Any dependent isomorphism in
P
is cocartesian.

3.

If
f
is a cocartesian arrow in
P
over an identity in
B
, then
f
is an isomorphism.

Proof 5.3
.

1.

Let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
with
b,b^{\prime}:B
, and
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
for
e:P\,b
and
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
. If
f
is an isomorphism, since
\widetilde{P}
is Segal, there exists a morphism
f^{-1}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},e)
over some morphism
u^{\prime}:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b)
such that
f^{-1}\circ f=\id_{e}
and
f\circ f^{-1}=\id_{e^{\prime}}
. But for the projection
\pi:\widetilde{P}\to B
, this implies

u^{\prime}\circ u=\pi(f^{-1})\circ\pi(f)=\pi(f^{-1}\circ f)=\pi(\id_{e})=\id_{b},

and likewise
u\circ u^{\prime}=\id_{b^{\prime}}
. Thus,
u^{\prime}\equiv:u^{-1}
is a two-sided inverse for
u
, so
u
is an isomorphism, because
B
is Segal (cf.
[
61
, Proposition 10.1]
). In particular,
\pi(f^{-1})=u^{-1}
implies
f^{-1}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u^{-1}}(e^{\prime},e)
.

2.

Let
b,b^{\prime}:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
and
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
such that
f
is an isomorphism. For
b^{\prime\prime\prime}:B
,
v:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime\prime})
consider a dependent morphism
g:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v\circ u}(e,e^{\prime\prime\prime})
. Since by
34
the type
\widetilde{P}
is Segal, both
u
and
f
are invertible , cf.
51
(
1
) and
[
61
, Proposition 1.10]
.

Define
h\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}g\circ f^{-1}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
. Then
h\circ f=(g\circ f^{-1})\circ f=g\circ\id_{e}=g
. This yields the desired filler.

For uniqueness, observe that for any
k:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime\prime})
s.t.
g=kf
we have
hf=kf
,
i.e.
,
h=k
applying
f^{-1}
.

3.

In general, arrows over an identity morphism are just arrows in a fiber (even definitionally). Thus, for
b:B
consider a cocartesian arrow
f:\hom_{P\,b}(e,e^{\prime})\equiv{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{\id_{b}}(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e,e^{\prime}:P\,b
.

Since
f
is cocartesian it has a retraction: there exists an arrow
v\colon b\to b
with
f^{\prime}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e)
, uniquely up to homotopy, s.t.
\id_{e}=f^{\prime}\circ f
. But this implies
\id_{b}=\pi(\id_{e})=\pi(f^{\prime})\circ\pi(f)=\pi(f^{\prime})
, so we can assume
f^{\prime}
to be over
\id_{b}
.

Since
f
is cocartesian, for any morphism
g:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{w}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime})
,
w:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})
with
g\circ f=f
we get that
g=\id_{e^{\prime}}
. Now, take
g:=ff^{\prime}
,
w:=\id_{b^{\prime}}
. Then
g\circ f=(f\circ f^{\prime})\circ f=f\circ(f^{\prime}\circ f)=f
, thus
f\circ f^{\prime}=\id_{e^{\prime}}
. Hence,
f
is an isomorphism.

5.1.2
Characterizations of cocartesian arrows

Proposition 52

(
[
39
, Definition 2.4.1.10]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family with total type
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
. Fix
b,b^{\prime}:B
and let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
be a morphism in
B
and
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
, for
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
, a dependent morphism.

The morphism
f
is cocartesian if and only if, for any
b^{\prime\prime}:B
,
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{E}(\langle b^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle,\langle b^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{E}(\langle b,e\rangle,\langle b^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ\langle u,f\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ u}

is a pullback.

Proof 5.4
.

By fibrant replacement, de-/strictification, and choice for extension types, we can replace the square in question by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{\sigma:\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[u,w]}\right\rangle}\sum_{h:e\to^{P}_{w}e^{\prime\prime}}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P(\sigma(t,s))\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[f,h]}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}(e\to_{w}^{P}e^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[u,w]}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})}}

Undwinding what it means for this square to be a pullback precisely recovers the condition that
f
be cocartesian.

We prove a characterization for cocartesian edges, recovering the definition established by Joyal and Lurie, and transferred to complete Segal spaces by Rasekh.

Let
B
be a
Segal type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Consider its total space
E:\equiv\sum_{b:B}P\,b
. For
b,b^{\prime}:B
, let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
an arrow with a dependent arrow
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{f}(e,e^{\prime})
above it, where
e:P\,b,e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
.

There is an induced commutative square involving comma objects, each of which can be described using extension types:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{\sigma:u\downarrow B}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}P(\sigma(t))\middle|^{\Delta_{1}^{1}}_{f}\right\rangle}\simeq f/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/B\simeq\mathrlap{\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Delta_{1}^{1}}_{u}\right\rangle}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{u:b\downarrow B}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}P(u(t))\middle|^{0}_{e}\right\rangle}\simeq e/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b/B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}

Proposition 53

(Joyal’s Criterion,
[
28
, Paragraph 20.4]
,
[
39
, Definition 2.4.1.1]
)
.

Let
B
be Segal and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family. Write
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and consider the canonical projection
\pi:E\to B
.

A dependent arrow
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
,
e:P\,b,e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
b,b^{\prime}:B
, is cocartesian if and only if the mediating map
\varphi
in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e/E\times_{b/B}u/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b/B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is an equivalence.

Proof 5.5
.

Note that by precondition both
B
and
E
are Segal types. The map
\varphi
is an equivalence if and only if

\prod_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\,\prod_{v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}\,\prod_{e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}}\,\prod_{h:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v\circ u}(e,e^{\prime\prime})}\,\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})}g\circ f=h\Big)

which is equivalent to
f
being cocartesian.

Corollary 54
.

For a Segal type
B
, any arrow in
B
is cocartesian in the unit family
\lambda b.\mathbf{1}:B\to\mathcal{U}
.

Another characterization familiar from the semantics is given in terms of the “cubical horn”. The pushout product of the two shape inclusions
b_{1}:\partial\Delta^{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
,
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
, can be depicted as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\partial\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1})\bigsqcup_{\partial\Delta^{1}\times\{0\}}(\Delta^{1}\times\{0\})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}

Proposition 55

(
[
39
, Proposition 2.4.1.8]
)
.

Let
f
be a dependent arrow in an isoinner family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a Rezk type
B
. Then
f
is cocartesian if and only if every diagram of the following form has a filler uniquely up to homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\partial\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1})\bigsqcup_{\partial\Delta^{1}\times\{0\}}\Delta^{1}\times\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We omit a proof, but it is straightforward to do, jumping between (degenerate) squares and
2
-simplices.

As has been pointed out to us by Emily Riehl, cocartesian arrows can also be characterized along similar lines like cocartesian families in
Theorems

5.10
and
5.8
and cocartesian functors in
Theorem

5.34
, using the formalism of relative adjunctions/absolute left lifting diagrams
[
63
, Theorem 5.1.7]
. Translating to the type-theoretic setting, this unwinds exactly to the above “cubical” formulation of the universal property. This viewpoint seems suitable for the type-theoretic setting and would also yield a more general account to the formal properties of cocartesian arrows, such as closure under composition or right cancelation. In this sense, cocartesian arrows can be seen as an instance of the notion of
j
-LARI cells
which is further and more explicitly discussed in
[
86
, Appendix A.2.1]
.

5.2
Cocartesian families

Cocartesian families are families of Rezk types such that every map in the base has a cocartesian lift w.r.t. a choice of the source vertex. After showing elementary properties such as functoriality we prove the Chevalley criterion which exhibits cocartesian families as LARI fibrations (w.r.t. the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
). By the results from
Section

3.2.2
it then follows that cocartesian fibrations are closed under pullback, composition, and dependent products. We proceed by giving three kinds of examples of cocartesian families: the comma codomain, the domain projection in case the base category has all pushouts, and the cocartesian replacement of an arbitrary map between Rezk types. Relating to the latter, we show that cocartesian replacement really satisfies the desired universal property.

Here, we are only concerned with
co-
cartesian fibrations, so we omit the dual case of right adjoint right inverse (RARI) adjunctions. Occasionally, reminiscent of the jargon of classical fibered
1
-category theory, we will refer to cocartesian fibrations simply as
opfibrations
(e.g., when talking about the
codomain opfibration
of a Rezk type).

5.2.1
Definition and basic properties

Definition 56

(Cocartesian lifting property)
.

A family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is said to
have (all) cocartesian lifts
if

{\mathrm{hasCocartLifts}}\,P:\equiv\prod_{b,b^{\prime}:B}\prod_{u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})}\prod_{e:P\,b}\sum_{e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}}\sum_{f:\hom^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}\,f.

Definition 57

(Cocartesian family)
.

For any type
B
, we call a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a
cocartesian family
if

{\mathrm{isCocartFam}}\,P:\equiv{\mathrm{isIsoInnerFam}}\,P\times{\mathrm{hasCocartLifts}}\,P.

If
B
is a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family, then any arrow
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
induces a functor
{u}^{P}_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b
defined by

{u}^{P}_{!}:\equiv\lambda d.\partial_{1}{P}_{!}({u},{d}).

We will often omit the superscript if the family is clear from the context.

Notation:
We often denote (types of) cocartesian arrows by a decorated arrow
. (Dually, cartesian arrows are denoted
.)

Definition 58

(Vertical arrow)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be an inner fibration over a Segal type. A dependent arrow
f:\Delta^{1}\to E
is called
vertical
if
\pi\,f
is an isomorphism.

Observe that, since being an isomorphism is a proposition in a Segal type by
[
61
, Proposition 1.10]
, being a vertical arrow is a proposition when in an inner family over a Segal type. In a cocartesian family one recovers the classically well-known fact that any dependent arrow factors as a cocartesian arrow followed by a vertical arrow.

Cocartesian families implement the idea of a
functorial
family of Rezk types,
i.e.
, in addition to transport along paths—which exists for arbitrary type families—there is also a notion of transport along
directed
arrows, which turns out to be compatible with the familiar path transport.

Proposition 59

(Functoriality)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family. For any
a:B
and
x:P\,a
there is an identity

{P}_{!}({\id_{a}},{x})=\id_{x},

and for any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
v:\hom_{B}(b,c)
, there is an identity

{P}_{!}({v\circ u},{x})={P}_{!}({v},{{u}_{!}(x)})\circ{P}_{!}({u},{x}).

Proof 5.6
.

The first claim follows from
51
(
3
), in combination with
45
.

The second claim follows from
50
(
1
).

Proposition 60
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family. For any arrow
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and elements
d:P\,a
,
e:P\,b
, we have equivalences between the types of (cocartesian) lifts of arrows from
d
to
e
and maps (isomorphisms, resp.) from
u_{!}d
to
e
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}

Proof 5.7
.

Consider the maps

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle:(d\to_{u}e)\longrightarrow(u_{!}d\to_{P\,b}e),\quad\Phi(f):\equiv\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(f),

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:(u_{!}d\to_{P\,b}e)\longrightarrow(d\to_{u}e),\quad\Psi(p):\equiv p\circ P_{!}(u,d).

From the universal property of cocartesian fillers, as argued in
46
, we find

\displaystyle\Phi(\Psi(g))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(g\circ P_{!}(u,d))=g,

\displaystyle\Psi(\Phi(h))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(h)\circ P_{!}(u,d)=h.

Now, if
u:d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }

is a cocartesian arrow by uniqueness of cocartesian lifts the filler
\Phi(u)
must be a path. Thus
\Phi
restricts to a map
\Phi^{\prime}:(d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{u}e)\longrightarrow(u_{!}d=_{P\,b}e)
.

If
p:u_{!}d=e
is a path, it is in particular cocartesian, so
\Psi(p)
is as well since cocartesian arrows are closed under composition. Hence,
\Psi
restricts to a map

\Psi^{\prime}:(u_{!}d=_{P\,b}e)\longrightarrow(d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

Thus, just as in the classical case, our cocartesian families capture the notion of covariantly functorial families of categories.

Due to
45
, over Rezk types being a cocartesian family is a proposition, and indeed this is the setting that we are interested in. In particular, cocartesian families over Rezk types are thus “cloven up to homotopy”. Given an arrow
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
together with
e:P\,b
, we write
P_{!}(u,e)
for the homotopically unique cocartesian lift of
u
. Even more, from the point of view of homotopy type theory, these cleavages are automatically “
split
’’.
21
21

21

This is understood in the sense analogous to
[
81
, Definition 3.1]
.

5.2.2
Characterizations of cocartesian families

We now turn to characterizations of cocartesian fibrations after
[
63
,
64
]
.

Cocartesian families via lifting

Theorem 5.8

(Chevalley criterion: Cocartesian families via lifting,
[
63
, Proposition 5.2.8(ii)]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family, and denote by
\pi:E\to B
the associated projection map. The family
P
is cocartesian if and only if the Leibniz cotensor map
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi:E^{\Delta^{1}}\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B
has a left adjoint right inverse:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof 5.9
.

Assume
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family. The gap map can be taken to be

i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,f.\langle u,\partial_{0}f\rangle:E^{\Delta^{1}}\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B.

For the candidate LARI we take the map that produces the cocartesian lifts,
i.e.
,

\chi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,e.\langle u,P_{!}(u,e)\rangle:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E^{\Delta^{1}}.

This is clearly a (strict) section of
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi
.

We show that for any
\langle u:b\to b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
in
\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B
and
\langle v:c\to c^{\prime},g:d\to d^{\prime}\rangle
in
E^{\Delta^{1}}
the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\Delta^{1}\to E}(\chi(u,e),\langle v,g\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\pi\downarrow B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,\partial_{0}\,g\rangle)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,g\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,g\rangle}}

defined by

\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,g\rangle}(k,m)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\pi\,k,\pi\,m,k\rangle,\quad\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,g\rangle}(w,r,k)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle k,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(g\circ k)\rangle

yield a quasi-transposing adjunction (cf.
Figure

7
for illustration).
22
22

22

For brevity, we shall henceforth leave the fixed parameters
\langle u,e\rangle,\langle v,g\rangle
implicit.

On the one hand, for a morphism
\langle w,r,k\rangle
we find

\Phi(\Psi(w,r,k))=\Phi(k,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(g\circ k))=\langle w,r,k\rangle

since
\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(g\circ k)
lies over
r
and
k
lies over
w
(even strictly so).

For a morphism
\langle k,m\rangle
we obtain

\Psi(\Phi(k,m))=\Psi(\pi\,k,\pi\,m,k)=\langle k,m\rangle

from the cocartesian universal property.

This shows
\chi\dashv i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi
is a LARI adjunction, as claimed.

Conversely, suppose
\chi
is a given LARI of
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi
, w.l.o.g. a strict section. Then, for any
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
,
c:B
,
e:P\,b
,
d:P\,c
, the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\Delta^{1}\to E}(\chi(u,e),\langle\id_{c},\id_{d}\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\pi\downarrow B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\id_{c},d\rangle)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

defined by

\Phi(w,v,h,g)\equiv\langle w,v,h\rangle

is an equivalence. Finally, contractibility of the fibers amounts to the cocartesian universal property of
\chi(u,e):e\to_{u}\partial_{1}(\chi(u,e))
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}\vphantom{b^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e\vphantom{c^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\,m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\,k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Cocartesian families via transport

There is another characterization of cocartesian families in terms of an adjointness condition. Any map
\pi:E\to B
between Rezk types is exhibited as a retract of the pullback map
\partial_{0}^{*}\pi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to B^{\Delta^{1}}
in the following way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The mediating map

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{\pi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\langle b,e\rangle.\langle\id_{b},e\rangle:E\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B

is a fibered functor from
\pi:E\to B
to the cocartesian replacement
23
23

23

Explicitly,
\partial^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,e.u(1):\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
, cf.
71
.
of
\partial_{0}^{*}\pi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to B^{\Delta^{1}}
,
i.e.
, there is a commutative triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{\prime}_{1}}

Denote the family of fibers of
\pi
by
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
. If
P
is cocartesian, it has “directed transport”

\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{\pi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{transp}_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,e.u^{P}_{!}e:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E.

This transport map
\tau
is easily checked to be a fibered functor from
\partial^{\prime}_{1}
to
\pi
. We will show that it is a fibered left adjoint of
\iota_{\pi}
, and conversely, the existence of a fibered left adjoint
\tau_{\pi}
to
\iota_{\pi}
will imply that
\pi
is cocartesian.

Theorem 5.10

(Cocartesian families via transport,
[
63
, Proposition 5.2.8(iii)]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family with associated total type projection
\pi:E\to B
.

Then,
P
is cocartesian if and only if the map

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{P}:E\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B,\quad\iota\,\langle b,e\rangle:\equiv\langle\id_{b},e\rangle

has a fibered left adjoint
24
24

24

cf.
126

\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{P}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E
as indicated in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof 5.11
.

Assume
P
is cocartesian. For the candidate left adjoint we take the map given by cocartesian transport

\tau:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E,\quad\tau(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\partial_{1}\,u,u_{!}e\rangle.

Then
\pi(\tau(u,e))\equiv\partial_{1}\,u\equiv\partial_{1}^{\prime}(u,e)
, so
\tau
is a fibered functor from
\partial_{1}^{\prime}
to
\pi
. We show that for any
\langle u:b\to b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
and
\langle c:B,d:P\,c\rangle
in
E
the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{E}(\langle b^{\prime},u_{!}e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\pi\downarrow B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\mathrm{id}_{c},d\rangle)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}}

defined by

\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}(v,g)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle vu,v,g\circ P_{!}(u,e)\rangle,\quad\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}(w,v,h)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)\rangle

form a quasi-equivalence (cf.
Figure

8
for illustration).
We have

\Phi(\Psi(w,v,h))=\Phi(v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h))=\langle vu,v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)\circ P_{!}(u,e)\rangle=\langle w,v,h\rangle

by
46
(
1
), and noting that for any square
\langle w,v\rangle:u\to\id_{c}
there is an identification
w=vu
.
Next, we find

\Psi(\Phi(v,g))=\Psi(vu,v,g\circ P_{!}(u,e))=\langle v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(g\circ P_{!}(u,e))\rangle=\langle v,g\rangle.

using again the properties of the fillers defined by the cocartesian lifts, cf.
46
,
2
.

So indeed
\tau
is left adjoint to
\iota
. Moreover, it is a fibered left adjoint as can be seen as follows. The unit is defined by

\eta_{\langle u,e\rangle}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi(\id_{b^{\prime}},\id_{u_{!}e})=\langle u,\id_{b^{\prime}},P_{!}(u,e)\rangle:\hom_{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\id_{b^{\prime}},u_{!}e\rangle).

Since the second component is an identity this is a vertical arrow in
\partial_{1}^{\prime}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
which proves the adjunction is fibered.

Suppose on the converse that
\tau\dashv_{B}\iota
is some fibered left adjunction. Since
\tau
is a fibered functor, for
\langle u:b\to b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
we can assume

\tau(u,e)\equiv\langle b^{\prime},\widehat{\tau}(u,e)\rangle.

Next,
\eta
being a
fibered
natural transformation fixes its part in
B
,
i.e.
, since in the square the lower horizontal edge has to be an identity the upper horizontal edge must be
u
(up to identification), so the only degree of freedom is the dependent arrow
f_{u,e}
as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\tau}(u,e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{u,e}:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Hence, we can assume

\eta_{u,e}\equiv\langle u,\id_{b^{\prime}},f_{u,e}\rangle.

By assumption, the transposing map induced by the unit

\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda v,g.\iota(v,g)\circ\eta_{u,e}\equiv\langle vu,v,g\circ f_{u,e}\rangle:\hom_{E}(\iota(u,e),\langle c,d\rangle)\longrightarrow\hom_{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\id_{c},d\rangle)

is an equivalence. Spelled out, this means for any
v:b^{\prime}\to c
,
h:e\to^{P}_{vu}d
there exists an arrow
g_{h}:\widehat{\tau}(u,e)\to_{v}d
, uniquely up to homotopy, s.t.
h=g_{h}\circ f_{u,e}
. This says exactly that
f_{u,e}:e\to\widehat{\tau}(u,e)
is a cocartesian lift of
u
w.r.t.
e
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c\vphantom{b^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c\vphantom{b^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ P_{!}(u,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Transposing maps of the fibered adjunction
\tau_{P}\dashv_{B}\iota_{P}

5.2.3
Closure properties of cocartesian families

In this section, when considering type families, we again assume the base types to be Rezk.

Stability of cocartesian families under composition follows as an instance of
27
.
In principle, the computation of the cocartesian lifts follows by instantiation and unpacking of the constructions given in the proofs of Proposition
27
and
123
, respectively. But actually, we are showing a stronger statement, in a direct way, where the condition on the second factor of the composition is being slightly weakened. The reason is that this will simplify a later proof about induced cocartesian fibrations between pullback types.

Proposition 61

(Identities are cocartesian fibrations)
.

For a (complete) Segal type
B
, the unit family
\lambda b.\mathbf{1}:B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian.

Proof 5.12
.

This is an easy consequence of
54
.

Proposition 62

(Cocartesian families are closed under the dependent product)
.

Let
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a family of Rezk types, and for each
i:I
, assume
P_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family. We denote
E_{i}:\equiv\widetilde{P_{i}}
. Then, the family associated to the map of sections

\prod_{i:I}E_{i}{\mathrel{\to}}\prod_{i:I}B_{i}

is a cocartesian family.

In particular, a cocartesian lift for
u:\alpha\to\beta
in
\prod_{i:I}B_{i}
w.r.t.
\sigma:\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(\alpha_{i})
is given by

\left(\prod_{i:I}P_{i}\right)_{!}(u,\sigma):\equiv\lambda i.\big(P(i)\big)_{!}(u(i),\sigma(i)).

Proof 5.13
.

Stability of cocartesian families under products follows as an instance of
25
. In principle, the computation of the cocartesian lifts follows by instantiating and unpacking the constructions given in the proofs of Propositions
25
and
121
, respectively.

More directly, one can alternatively take the suggested candidate lift in the product fibration and show that it is indeed cocartesian. But this follows from employing the universal property pointwisely and then invoking function extensionality.

Corollary 63

(Cocartesian families are closed under exponentiation)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family. For any shape or type
x:X
, the family
P^{X}
is cocartesian.

63
can be seen as a shadow of the fact that cocartesian fibrations are
representably defined
, cf.
[
63
, Section 5.6]
.
To fully express this, however, we would need to employ modal type theory.

Definition 64

(Partial cocartesian families)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family over a Rezk type
B
. An isoinner family
Q:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
is called
partial cocartesian (w.r.t.
P
)
if every
P
-cocartesian arrow in
\widetilde{P}
has a
Q
-cocartesian lift (w.r.t. a given point in the fiber over the source).

Proposition 65

(Composite cocartesian fibrations,
[
63
, Lemma 5.2.3]
)
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
\pi
is a cocartesian fibration. Then the composite
\rho\equiv\pi\circ\xi
is a cocartesian fibration, whenever
\xi
is a partial cocartesian fibration over
\pi
.

Call
P
the straightening of
\pi
,
Q
the straightening of
\xi
, and
R
the straightening of the composite
\rho
. In particular, up to homotopy the cocartesian lift for
u:a\to b
in
B
w.r.t. to the pair
e:P\,a
,
x:Q\,a\,e
is given by

(P\odot Q)_{!}(u,\langle e,x\rangle):\equiv Q_{!}(P_{!}(u,e),x).

Proof 5.14
.

Consider an arrow
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
with lifts
f:e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

conclude:

Corollary 66
.

The composite of two cocartesian fibration is itself a cocartesian fibration.

Proposition 67

(Cocartesian families are closed under pullback)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family, and
k:A\to B
be a map. Then the pullback family
k^{*}P:A\to\mathcal{U}
with associated projection
k^{*}\pi
in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a cocartesian family.

In particular, a cocartesian lift of
u:a\to a^{\prime}
w.r.t. to
e:P\,ka
is given by

(k^{*}P)_{!}(u,e):\equiv P_{!}(ku,e).

Proof 5.15
.

Stability of cocartesian families under products follows as an instance of
Theorem

3.12
. The computation of the cocartesian lifts follows by instantiation and unpacking of the constructions given in the proofs of Propositions
3.12
and
122
, respectively.

5.2.4
Examples of cocartesian families

We give three important kinds of examples of cocartesian fibrations: comma opfibrations (including codomain opfibrations), domain opfibrations, and free cocartesian fibrations. In a setting including universe types which are Rezk, one would naturally be interested in
universal op-/fibrations
, arising as co-/domain projections associated to the categorical universes.

Proposition 68

(Comma opfibration)
.

Let
g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f
be a cospan of Rezk types. Then the codomain projection from the comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}

is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof 5.16
.

We show that, for
v:c\to c^{\prime}
in
C
, and
\alpha:fb\to gc
in
f\downarrow g
the candidate for the cocartesian lift is defined as the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gc}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{gc^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}gv\circ\alpha}

For any
v^{\prime}:c^{\prime}\to c^{\prime\prime}
we are to consider a dependent arrow over
v^{\prime}v
in the comma object with domain
\alpha
. This amounts to giving
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
and
\beta:fb^{\prime}\to gc^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
\beta\circ fu=g(v^{\prime}\circ v)\circ\alpha
. As indicated in the figure below, this represents the outer rectangle, and we have to uniquely exhibit a square, on the inner right, as a filler:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{fb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gc}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{gc^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{gc^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gv^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(v^{\prime}\circ v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Clearly the horizontal arrows can be chosen as
fu
and
gv^{\prime}
, resp., by the conditions read off from the adjacent triangles. Since by assumption, there is an identity
\beta\circ fu=g(v^{\prime}v)\circ\alpha
, hence
gv^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime}=\beta\circ fu
, so the square on the right is indeed commutative, and moreover the uniquely determined filler.

Corollary 69

(Codomain opfibration)
.

For any Rezk type
B
, the projection

\partial_{1}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,\penalty\ \partial_{1}(f):\equiv f(1)

is a cocartesian fibration, called the
codomain opfibration
.

Directly by
47
we obtain that the domain projection of a Rezk type is a cocartesian fibration given that the base has pushouts.

Proposition 70

(Domain opfibration)
.

If
B
is a Rezk type that has all pushouts, then the domain projection

\partial_{0}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,\quad\partial_{0}(u):\equiv u(0)

is a cocartesian fibration.

5.2.5
Towards monadicity: the free cocartesian family

As discussed in
[
3
,
22
,
62
]
cocartesian fibrations are monadic (and comonadic) over general functors (over a fixed base). This means that for any functor
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
there is a
free cocartesian fibration

L(\pi):L(E)\twoheadrightarrow B
. Due to the current absence of categorical universes in our type theory we postpone a discussion with emphasis on a global perspective similar to the cited works. However, we can still state and prove the universal property for this construction, so that in future work, in presence of the desired universes the actual monadicity statement will easily follow.
Namely, we define a “unit map”
\iota_{\pi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota:\pi\to_{B}L(\pi)
, and prove that precomposition constitutes an equivalence of types
26
26

26

In general,
{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(\pi,\xi)
is the
\Sigma
-type of fiberwise maps from
\pi
to
\xi
which preserve cocartesian lifts. Cf. Subsection
5.3
for a more thorough treatment.

-\circ\iota_{P}:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi),

for any
cocartesian
fibration
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
.

Definition 71

(Free cocartesian family,
[
22
, Definition 4.1]
,
[
3
, Terminology 3.3.6]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Then the family

L(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\sum_{u:B\mathbin{\downarrow}b}P(\partial_{0}u):B\to\mathcal{U}

is the
free cocartesian family associated to
P
, or the
cocartesian replacement of
P
.

In more categorical terms, the free cocartesian family—in its incarnation as a fibration—is constructed by first pulling back the map
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
along the domain projection, and then postcomposing with the
codomain
projection:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L(\pi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}

Morphisms in the cocartesian replacement can be depicted as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

We will see that, indeed the free cocartesian family is a cocartesian family.

Theorem 5.17

(Cocartesian replacement is cocartesian,
[
22
, Theorem 4.3]
,
[
3
, Lemma 3.3.1]
)
.

If
B
is a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family, then the family
L(P):B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian.

Proof 5.18
.

By the closure properties of isoinner families, since
P
is an isoinner family, so is
L(P)
.

Let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
be an arrow in
B
, and
\langle v,e\rangle:LP(b)
a point over
b
, where
v:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and
e:P\,a
.

We define the candidate lift to be
\langle\id_{a},u,\id_{e}\rangle:{\mathrm{hom}}^{LP}_{u}(\langle v,e\rangle,\langle vu,e\rangle)
,
i.e.
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

One can readily verify that this arrow is
LP
-cocartesian.
27
27

27

Cf.
69
.
Namely, for
u^{\prime}:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, let
t:\hom_{B}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
,
e^{\prime}:P\,a^{\prime}
, together with

and

s.t.
t\circ w=(u^{\prime}u)\circ v
. We find the ensuing filler over
v
as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}

By construction, the dashed arrows are unique up to homotopy.

For example, the free fibration of the identity
\id_{B}:B\twoheadrightarrow B
is the codomain opfibration
\partial_{1}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow B
(cf.
[

, Example 4.2]
).

We now demonstrate how the cocartesian replacement is in fact a ‘‘free’’ construction.

With categorical universes at hand, one would obtain statements more closely resembling

We take as the “unit map”

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}

Proposition 72

(Universal property of cocartesian replacement, cf.
[
22
, Theorem 4.5]
,
[
3
, Corollary 3.3.4]
)
.

For a Rezk type
B
, consider an isoinner fibration
\pi:E\to B
, and a cocartesian fibration
\xi:F\to B
. Then the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi\;{\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}\;{{-}\circ\iota_{P}}}

is an equivalence of types.

Proof 5.19
.

Denote by
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
the straightenings of
\pi:E\to B
and
\xi:F\to B
, resp.

We aim to give a quasi-inverse of the precomposition map. Let

\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\varphi.\varphi^{\prime}:\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\to{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)

where

\varphi^{\prime}_{b}(v,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e),

for
v:a\to b
,
e:P\,a
.
First, we are to show that this operation is really valued in cocartesian functors.
For this, we have to show that, for any
v:a\to b
,
e:P\,a
, the arrow

\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,e):v_{!}Q(\varphi_{b}\,e)\longrightarrow^{P}_{u}(uv)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,a)

is
Q
-cocartesian. To that end, we observe the following. Let
a:B
,
d:Q\,a
be fixed. Consider the maps
\mathrm{cst}(d),\tau_{Q}(-,d):a\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E
defined by

\mathrm{cst}(d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}d,\quad\tau_{Q}(-,d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v^{Q}_{!}(d):d.

We define the natural transformation

Q_{!}(-,d):\underset{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E}{\hom}(\mathrm{cst}(d),\tau_{Q}(-,d)),\quad Q_{!}(-,d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}Q_{!}(v,d):d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
