Morphisms in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
are given by commutative triangles
u:v\to w
, so for fixed
v
the type of morphisms in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
starting at
v
is equivalent to the type
(\partial_{1}\,v)\mathbin{\downarrow}B
. Hence, any morphism in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
can be taken to be of the form
u:v\to uv
, for
v:a\to b
,
u:b\to b^{\prime}
. The naturality squares of
Q_{!}(-,d)
thus are of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(uv)_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)}

Note that the lower vertical arrow is given by

\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)^{Q}_{!}(d)=(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d).

By right cancelation,
(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)
is cocartesian, and hence we have an identity of arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(uv)_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{Q}_{!}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{Q}_{!}(v_{!}^{Q}(d))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{!}(u,v_{!}^{Q}(d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)}

In the cocartesian replacement
\partial_{1}^{\prime}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
, the cocartesian lift of
u:b\to b^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\langle v:a\to b,e:P\,a\rangle
is given by
\langle\id_{a},u,\id_{e}\rangle
. Now, by the previous discussion we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{u}(\id_{a},u,\id_{e})

\displaystyle=\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e)

\displaystyle=(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}\,e)

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(u,v^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}\,e))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(u,\varphi^{\prime}_{a}(v,e))

which shows that
\varphi^{\prime}:L(\pi)\to_{B}\xi
is a cocartesian functor, as desired.

We now turn to showing that precomposing with
\iota
gives an equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\simeq{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi).

We define

\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\psi.\iota^{*}\psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\psi.\psi\circ\iota:\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\to{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi),

and recall that in the converse direction

\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\varphi.\varphi^{\prime}:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)\to\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)

with
\varphi^{\prime}_{b}(v,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e)
.
Let
\psi:L\,\pi\to_{B}F
be a cocartesian functor.
We compute

(\iota^{*}\psi)_{b}^{\prime}(v,e)=v_{!}^{Q}(\iota^{*}\psi_{a}(e))=v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e)).

Since
\psi
is cocartesian, we have
v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v_{!}^{LP}(\id_{a},e))
, cf.
78
. Now, the
LP
-cocartesian lift of
v:a\to b
w.r.t.
\langle\id_{a},e\rangle
is given by
\langle\id_{a},v,\id_{e}\rangle:\langle\id_{a},e\rangle\to\langle v,e\rangle
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

As a dependent arrow in
LP
, the codomain of this morphism is the pair
\langle v,e\rangle
. In sum, this means

v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v_{!}^{LP}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v,e)=(\iota^{*}\psi)_{b}^{\prime}(v,e),

i.e.
,
\Psi(\Phi(\psi))
.
On the other hand, for an arbitrary fiberwise map
\varphi
from
P
to
Q
, we find that

(\iota^{*}\varphi)^{\prime}(b,e)=\varphi_{b}^{\prime}(\id_{b},e)=(\id_{b})_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,e)=\varphi_{b}(e)

since cocartesian lifts of identities are themselves identities. This gives
\Phi(\Psi(\varphi))=\varphi
.

Remark 73
.

In fact, there also exists a discrete version of co-/cartesian replacement, cf. e.g.
[
3
, Section 4.3]
. In type theory, this construction demands some more involvement, since it will include fiberwise localization.

5.3
Cocartesian functors

Cocartesian functors are an approproiate notion of morphism between cocartesian fibrations (not necessarily over the same base). They are defined as fiberwise maps that preserve cocartesian morphisms. We discuss several closure properties of cocartesian functors coming from
\infty
-cosmos theory. We also transfer the alternative characterizations of cocartesian functors from
[
63
, Theorem 5.3.4]
, reworking the necessary tools from formal category theory in
Sections

A.1
and
A.2
.

5.3.1
Morphisms of sections

We commence this section with a brief discussion of morphisms between sections. This willl turn out to be particularly important in the context of the Yoneda Lemma in
Section

7
.

First, let
B
be any type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
any family. For any morphism
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
a,b:B
, there is an induced dependent morphism

\sigma\,u:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(\sigma\,a,\sigma\,b),\quad\sigma\,u:\equiv\lambda t.\sigma(u(t)).

Consider sections
\sigma,\tau:\prod_{B}P
and a morphism
\kappa:\hom_{\prod_{B}P}(\sigma,\tau)
. Again, we imagine
\kappa
as a kind of
2
-cell, and abbreviate
\hom_{\prod_{B}P}(\sigma,\tau)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(\sigma\Rightarrow^{P}\tau)=(\sigma\Rightarrow\tau)
.

For any
x:B
,
\kappa
induces a vertical morphism

\kappa\,x:\hom_{P\,x}(\sigma\,x,\tau\,x),\quad\kappa\,x:\equiv\lambda t.\kappa(t,x).

By the axiom of choice, we have

(\Delta^{1}\to\prod_{B}P)\simeq\sum_{u:\Delta^{1}\to B}(\Delta^{1}\to P(u)).

Let
B
is a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family. Then
\kappa
acts on arrows in the base
B
in the sense that in
\prod_{B}P
there are canonical squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

and these squares compose over composable morphisms.
29
29

29

But we will not discuss the validity of the Segal condition for
\Pi
-types here.

5.3.2
Definition and basic properties

Definition 74

(Fiberwise maps)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
be families. A
fiberwise map
or
fibered functor from
P
to
Q
is a pair of functions
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle
, where

•

j:B\to C
,

•

\varphi:\prod_{b:B}(P\,b\to Q\,j(b))
.

We call
\Phi
a
fibered equivalence
if both
j
and
\varphi
are equivalences.

We write
{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)\equiv(P{\mathrel{\to}}Q)\equiv(\pi_{P}{\mathrel{\to}}\pi_{Q})
for the ensuing type of fiberwise maps. In the case of
B\equiv C
, we denote this type as
{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B}(P,Q)=(P\to_{B}Q)=(\pi_{P}\to_{B}\pi_{Q})
.

Note that (by fibrant replacement) the type of commutative squares is equivalent to the type of maps between families.

Observe that given a map between families
P
and
Q
as above we get a strictly commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{total}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Q}}

For any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
, the fiberwise map
\Phi
acts on arrows over
u
in the following way. For
f:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(d,e)
,
d:Pa
,
e:Pb
, we have

\varphi_{u}(f):\equiv\lambda t.\varphi_{u(t)}(f(t)):{\mathrm{hom}}^{Q}_{ju}(\varphi_{a}(d),\varphi_{b}(e)).

Definition 75

(Cocartesian functors)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
be cocartesian families over Rezk types. Given a fibered functor
\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle j,\varphi\rangle
from
P
to
Q
, if

\Phi=\total(\varphi):\widetilde{P}\to\widetilde{Q},\quad\Phi_{b}(e):\equiv\langle j(b),\varphi_{j\,b}(e)\rangle

preserves cocartesian arrows, then we call
\Phi
a
cocartesian functor
:
30
30

30

This is a proposition because being a cocartesian arrow is a proposition in our usual setting.

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\Phi):\equiv\prod_{\langle u,f\rangle:\Delta^{1}\to\widetilde{P}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{u}^{P}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{ju}^{Q}(\varphi f).

We define

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,C}(P,Q):\equiv\sum_{\Phi:{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\Phi).

For cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
to
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a common base, we have

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\varphi)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:\Delta^{1}\to B\\
f:(t:\Delta^{1})\to P(u\,t)\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{P}_{u}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{Q}_{u}(\varphi f)

and write

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,Q)\simeq\sum_{\varphi:P\to_{B}Q}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\varphi).

Definition 76

(Cocartesian sections)
.

If the unstraightening of
P
is an identity,
\Phi
can be identified with its second component, which in turn is the same as a section of
Q
. In this case, if
\Phi
is a cocartesian functor it is called a
cocartesian section
. For a section
\sigma:\prod_{B}P
, this condition amounts to

{\mathrm{isCocartSect}}_{P}(\sigma):\equiv\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:\Delta^{1}\to B\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{u}^{P}(\sigma\circ u).

We denote by

\prod_{B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}P:\equiv\sum_{\sigma:\prod_{B}P}{\mathrm{isCocartSect}}_{P}(\sigma)

the subtype of cocartesian sections.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau\circ\sigma}

Figure 9:
The two equivalent fibrations
\partial_{0}:(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}\to\Delta^{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\Delta^{2}\to\Delta^{1}

In general, cocartesian families can have non-cocartesian sections. This implies that fiberwise maps between cocartesian families are not automatically cocartesian. As an example, consider the cocartesian fibration

\lx@nobreakspace\partial_{0}:(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}\to\Delta^{1},\quad\partial_{0}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda f.f(0)

which is fibered equivalent to:

\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\Delta^{2}\to\Delta^{1},\quad\mathrm{pr}_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\langle t,s\rangle.t

The elements of
(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}
are given by

\lx@nobreakspace f_{k}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.k,\quad g\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.t,

for
k=0,1
, and the non-identity morphisms are the squares
\sigma
,
\tau
and
\tau\circ\sigma
, defined as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

Cf.
Figure

9
for a visualization of the two equivalent fibrations
\partial_{0}:(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta^{1}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\Delta^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta^{1}
(for the latter, using the choice of coordinates and embeddings from
[
61
, Subsection 2.3]
, cf.
Section

2.2
).

The inclusion of
\Delta^{1}
into
\Delta^{2}
as the “long edge”
\langle 0,0\rangle\to\langle 1,1\rangle
(corresponding to
\tau\circ\sigma
in
(\Delta^{1})^{\Delta^{1}}
) is a non-cocartesian section of
\pi
since there is no map
\langle 1,1\rangle\to\langle 0,1\rangle
.

Proposition 77

(Naturality of cocartesian liftings)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
cocartesian families, and
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)
a cocartesian functor. Then
\Phi
commutes with cocartesian lifts,
i.e.
, for any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
there is an identification of arrows

\varphi\big(P_{!}(u,d)\big)=_{\Delta^{1}\to(ju)^{*}Q}Q_{!}(ju,\varphi_{a}d)

and hence of endpoints

\varphi_{b}(u_{!}^{P}d)=_{Q(jb)}(ju)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}d).

In particular there is a homotopy commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{P}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ju)}^{Q}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{b}}

Proof 5.20
.

For
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and
d:P\,a
, consider the
P
-cocartesian lift
{P}_{!}({u},{d}):{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(d,{u}^{P}_{!}(d))
. Since
\varphi
is a cocartesian functor the arrow
\varphi_{u}({P}_{!}({u},{d})):{\mathrm{hom}}^{Q}_{ju}(\varphi_{a}d,\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d)))
is
Q
-cocartesian.

On the other hand,
{Q}_{!}({ju},{\varphi_{a}d}):{\mathrm{hom}}^{Q}_{ju}(\varphi_{a}d,{ju}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}d))
is as well a
Q
-cocartesian lift of
ju
with domain
\varphi_{a}d
, thus coincides with
\varphi_{u}({P}_{!}({u},{d}))
up to a path, in particular this gives an identification
\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d))={ju}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}d)
.

Corollary 78

(Naturality over a common base (
[
63
, Exercise 5.3.iii]
; discrete case in sHoTT:
[
61
, Proposition 8.17]
))
.

Consider a Rezk type
B
, cocartesian families
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
, and a cocartesian functor
\varphi:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,Q)
. Then
\varphi
commutes with the actions of arrows,
i.e.
, for any
a,b:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
d:P(a)
, we get an
identification

\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d))=_{Qb}{u}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}(d)),

thus a homotopy commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{P}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{Q}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{b}}

5.3.3
Closure properties of cocartesian functors

The closure properties stated in this section are to be understood w.r.t. the (non-full) sub-
(\infty,1)
-category of the
(\infty,1)
-category of arrows which has as objects cocartesian fibrations
E\twoheadrightarrow B
and as arrows cocartesian functors between those. In the absence of categorical universes, we capture the proclaimed limits and cotensors by spelling out type-theoretically their universal properties.

We start by explaining horizontal and vertical composition of cocartesian functors, alluding to the fact that in the model cocartesian fibrations form an
(\infty,1)
-double category.

In the following, for a fibered functor
\varphi
, we often write
\widetilde{\varphi}
for its totalization.

Proposition 79

(Horizontal and vertical composition of cocartesian functors,
[
63
, Exercise 5.3.ii]
)
.

1.

Cocartesian functors compose horizontally: Suppose, we are given cocartesian families
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
R:C\to\mathcal{U}
over Rezk types
A,B,C
. If
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{A,B}(P,Q)
,
\Psi\equiv\langle k,\psi\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,C}(Q,R)
are cocartesian functors, then the
horizontal composite

\Psi\circ_{\mathrm{h}}\Phi:\equiv\langle k\circ j,\psi\circ\varphi\rangle
defines a cocartesian functor from
P
to
R
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ j}

2.

Cocartesian functors compose vertically: Suppose, we are given cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
P^{\prime}:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q^{\prime}:\widetilde{Q}\to\mathcal{U}
, over Rezk types
A
and
B
. If
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,A}(P,Q)
,
\Psi\equiv\langle\widetilde{\varphi},\psi\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,A}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
are cocartesian functors, then the
vertical composite

\Phi{\circ_{\mathrm{v}}}\Psi:\equiv\langle j,\psi\rangle
defines a cocartesian functor from
R\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Sigma_{P}Q
to
R^{\prime}:\equiv\Sigma_{P^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
:
31
31

31

We have
\Sigma_{P}Q\equiv Q\odot P
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{Q^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}\circ\xi^{\prime}}

Proof 5.21
.

Recall
Section

5.2.3
for the closure properties of cocartesian fibrations, and how to compute cocartesian lifts in the respective constructions.

1.

Fiberwise composition of the fiberwise maps
\varphi:\prod_{a:A}P\,a\to Q\,ja
,
\psi:\prod_{b:B}Q\,b\to R\,kb
is given by

\displaystyle\psi\circ\varphi:\equiv\lambda a.\psi_{ja}\circ\varphi_{a}:\prod_{a:A}P\,a\to R\,kja.

(5.3)

Since
\varphi
and
\psi
both are cocartesian functors, for arrows
u:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
,
v:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
, and points
e:P\,a
,
d:Q\,b
, there are paths:

\displaystyle\varphi_{u}(P_{!}(u,e))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(ju,\varphi_{a}e)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\psi_{v}(Q_{!}(v,d))

\displaystyle=R_{!}(kv,\psi_{b}d)

(5.5)

Using these identifications, we find

\displaystyle(\psi\circ\varphi)_{u}(P_{!}(u,e))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:fibw-comp}}}{{=}}\psi_{ju}(\varphi_{u}(P_{!}(u,e)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:phi-hor-cocart}}}{{=}}\psi_{ju}(Q_{!}(ju,\varphi_{a}e))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:psi-hor-cocart}}}{{=}}R_{!}(kju,\psi_{kja}(\varphi_{a}e))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:fibw-comp}}}{{=}}R_{!}(kju,(\psi\circ\varphi)(a)).

2.

Let
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
,
u^{\prime}:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
, be arrows with points
e:P\,b
,
\langle e,d\rangle:Q(b,e)
, and
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
. First, recall that lifts in the composite families are given by:

\displaystyle R_{!}(u,e,d)

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(P_{!}(u,e),d)

(5.6)

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{!}(u^{\prime},e^{\prime},d^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}_{!}(P^{\prime}_{!}(u^{\prime},e^{\prime}),d^{\prime})

(5.7)

Since
\varphi
and
\psi
are cocartesian functors, there are identifications:

\displaystyle\varphi(P_{!}(u,e))

\displaystyle=P_{!}^{\prime}(ju,\varphi_{b}(e))

(5.8)

\displaystyle\psi(Q_{!}(u,f,e,d))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}^{\prime}(ju,\varphi_{u}(f),\varphi_{b}(e),\psi_{\varphi_{b}(e)}(d))

(5.9)

This gives a path

\displaystyle\psi(R_{!}(u,e,d))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:comp-lifts}}}{{=}}\psi(Q_{!}(P_{!}(u,e),e,d))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:psi-vert-cocart}}}{{=}}Q^{\prime}_{!}(ju,\varphi_{u}P_{!}(u,e),\varphi_{b}e,\psi_{\varphi_{b}e}d)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:phi-vert-cocart}}}{{=}}Q^{\prime}_{!}(ju,P_{!}^{\prime}(ju,\varphi_{b}e),\varphi_{b}e,\psi_{\varphi_{b}e}d)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:comp'-lifts}}}{{=}}R_{!}^{\prime}(ju,\varphi_{b}e,\psi_{\varphi_{b}e}d).

Proposition 80

(Product cones are cocartesian functors)
.

Let
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a type family over an arbitrary type
I
, and
P_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
cocartesian families with total types
E_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P_{i}}
. Then, for any
k:I
the projections from the product fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{k}}}

are cocartesian.

Proof 5.22
.

The projection maps are given by evaluation at
i
. From the description of the cocartesian arrows in
62
it follows that this defines a cocartesian functor.

Proposition 81

(Cocartesian universality of products)
.

Let
I
be a small type,
B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be a family, and
P:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
a family such that for all
i:I
the family
P_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family. We denote the associated projections by
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}
.

Then the product fibration
\prod_{i}E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i}B_{i}
satisfies the following universal property: For any cocartesian family
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
, with associated projection
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
, given a family of cocartesian functors
\langle\alpha_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle:\xi{\mathrel{\to}}\pi_{k}
for
k:I
, there exists a unique cocartesian functor, the
target tupling

\langle(\alpha_{i})_{i:I},(\psi_{i})_{I:I}\rangle:\xi{\mathrel{\to}}\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}
s.t. that every diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{i})_{i:I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{i})_{i:I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

commutes.

Proof 5.23
.

We only point out that the square given by
\langle(\alpha_{i})_{i:I},(\psi_{i})_{i:I}\rangle
is cocartesian. But this is again clear, because cocartesian lifts in the product fibration are defined pointwisely.

Proposition 82

(Pullback squares are cocartesian functors, cf
[
63
, Example 5.3.3]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family with projection
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
. For any map
k:A\to B
, the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.24
.

The square acts as

\langle k,\varphi\rangle(v,d)=\langle kv,d\rangle.

By
67
, the pullback map
k^{*}\pi:k^{*}E\to A
is a cocartesian fibration.

We abbreviate
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}k^{*}P
. Let
v:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
be an arrow and
d:k^{*}P(a)\equiv P(ka)
a point in the fiber. Again by
67
, the
Q
-cocartesian lift is given by

Q_{!}(v,d)=P_{!}(kv,d).

This leads to

\varphi(v,Q_{!}(v,d))=\langle kv,Q_{!}(v,d)\rangle=\langle kv,P_{!}(kv,d)\rangle=\langle kv,P_{!}(kv,\varphi d)\rangle.

Proposition 83

(Pullback cones are cocartesian functors)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
P^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U}
, and
P^{\prime\prime}:B^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{U}
be cocartesian families with associated projection maps
\pi:E\to B
,
\pi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
, and
\pi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}
, resp.

Consider a commutative cubical diagram as below, where the given vertical maps are cocartesian fibrations and the front and right square are cocartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}\times_{\pi}\pi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

Then the mediating map

\pi^{\prime\prime\prime}:\equiv\pi^{\prime}\times_{\pi}\pi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}:\equiv E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}\equiv:B^{\prime\prime\prime}

is a cocartesian fibration. Furthermore, the back and the left square are cocartesian functors.

Specifically, the cocartesian lift of an arrow
u^{\prime\prime\prime}:\equiv\langle u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\rangle
w.r.t.
\langle e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\rangle
is given by

\langle P_{!}(u,e),P_{!}(u^{\prime},e^{\prime}),P_{!}(u^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\rangle.

Proof 5.25
.

Denote by
Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}:B\to\mathcal{U}
the families associated to the maps
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
The map in question is equivalent to (cf.
[
68
, Theorem 24.2.3(ii)]
) the following projection:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime\prime\prime}:\equiv\sum_{b^{\prime\prime\prime}:B^{\prime\prime\prime}}P^{\prime}\,b^{\prime}\times_{P\,b}P^{\prime\prime}\,b^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime\prime\prime}\simeq\sum_{b:B}Q^{\prime}\,b\times_{Q\,b}Q^{\prime\prime}\,b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime\prime\prime}}

The candidate
\pi^{\prime\prime\prime}
-cocartesian lift of
\langle u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\rangle
w.r.t.
\langle e:P(u0),e^{\prime}:P^{\prime}(u^{\prime}0),e^{\prime\prime}:P^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime\prime}0)\rangle
is given by

\langle P_{!}(u,e),P_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},e^{\prime}),P_{!}^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\rangle,

and since the universal property is satisfied fiberwisely it is satisfied in the pullback fibration.

.

Given a commutative cube of cocartesian fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}

Proof 5.26
.

By fibrant replacement, we can take the whole diagram to commute strictly. This gives a cocartesian functor comprised of the dependent pair

\alpha\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\beta^{\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime}\equiv\beta^{\prime\prime}\circ\alpha^{\prime\prime}:A\to B,\quad\psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi^{\prime}\circ\psi^{\prime}\equiv\varphi^{\prime\prime}\circ\psi^{\prime\prime}:F\to E.

Then
\alpha^{\prime\prime\prime}
acts as the fiber product tupling
\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\rangle
, and likewise
\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is given by
\langle\psi,\psi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime}\rangle
. Denote by
R:A\to\mathcal{U}
the family associated to the fibration
\xi:F\to A
. For an arrow
v:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
and a point
d:R\,a
, the
R
-cocartesian lift gets mapped to

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(R_{!}(v,d))

\displaystyle=\langle\psi(R_{!}(v,d)),\psi^{\prime}(R^{\prime}_{!}(v,d)),\psi^{\prime\prime}(R_{!}(v,d))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P_{!}(\alpha v,\psi d),P_{!}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}v,\psi^{\prime}d),P_{!}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha^{\prime\prime}v,\psi^{\prime\prime}d)\rangle

(
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}
cocart. functors
)

\displaystyle=P^{\prime\prime\prime}_{!}(\alpha\,v,\psi\,d),

(
construction of lifts by
83
)

which establishes the claim.

Proposition 85

(Sequential limit cones are cocartesian functors)
.

Consider an inverse diagram of cocartesian fibrations as below where all of the connecting squares are cocartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Then the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
between the limit types is a cocartesian fibration, and the projections constitute cocartesian functors.

Proof 5.27
.

Note that we can present the limiting types as pullbacks in the following way: First, for the base types, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\beta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\beta_{n}=f_{n}(\beta_{n+1})\simeq B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

since

\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n}\simeq\sum_{\beta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\beta_{2k+1}=f_{2k+1}(\beta_{2k+2}),\quad\text{and}\quad\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n+1}\simeq\prod_{\beta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\beta_{2k}=f_{2k}(\beta_{2k+1}).

Lying over, the limiting total type arises as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\langle\beta,\sigma\rangle:B_{\infty}}\sum_{\vartheta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}P_{n}(\beta_{n})}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\vartheta_{n}=_{\sigma_{n}}g_{n}(\vartheta_{n+1})\simeq E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

because

\displaystyle\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\langle\beta,\sigma\rangle:B_{2n}}\sum_{\vartheta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}P_{n}(\beta_{n})}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\vartheta_{2k+1}=_{\sigma_{2k+1}}g_{2k+1}(\vartheta_{2k+2})

\displaystyle\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n+1}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\langle\beta,\sigma\rangle:B_{2n+1}}\sum_{\vartheta:\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}P_{n}(\beta_{n})}\prod_{k:\mathbb{N}}\vartheta_{2k}=_{\sigma_{2k}}g_{2k}(\vartheta_{2k+1})

Since cocartesian fibrations are closed under composition by
65
and dependent products by
62
, and the induced maps are cocartesian functors by
80
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

\scriptstyle-

(5.10)

shows that the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
is a cocartesian fibration, too, by
83
.

Finally, coming back to the sequential limit of squares, it is clear that the mediating squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}

consisting of evaluations are cocartesian functors.

Proposition 86

(Cocartesian universality of sequential limits)
.

Given an inverse diagram of cocartesian fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}

and a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n+1}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{n+1}}

lying above, there exists uniquely up to homotopy a cocartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{\infty}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{i:I}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim_{i:I}k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}

together with homotopies as indicated in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}

Proof 5.28
.

The type of cones over a fixed inverse diagram of squares is equivalent to the type of cones over the ensuing cube (
5.10
). Then the claim follows by
84
.

Proposition 87

(Cocartesian functors are closed under dependent products)
.

For a type
I:\mathcal{U}
, suppose there are families
A,B:I\to\mathcal{U}
with families of families
P,Q:\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
such that
P_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q_{i}:A_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
are cocartesian for each
i:I
. Then, writing
\xi_{i}:F_{i}\to A_{i}
and
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\to B_{i}
, resp., for the associated projections, given a family of cocartesian functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{i}}

the square induced by taking the dependent product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}F_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}A_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}B_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\xi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}k_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.29
.

Recall that cocartesian lifts in the product families are computed pointwise. For an arrow
\sigma:\alpha\Rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}
of sections in
\prod_{i:I}A_{i}
and a section
\vartheta:\prod_{i:I}\alpha^{*}P_{i}
lying over we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}\big)\big((\prod_{i:I}\xi_{i})_{!}(\sigma,\vartheta)\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda i.\varphi_{i}(Q_{i})_{!}(\sigma_{i},\vartheta_{i})=\lambda i.(P_{i})_{!}(k_{i}\sigma_{i},\varphi_{i}\vartheta_{i}))

\displaystyle=\big(\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}\big)_{!}(\kappa\sigma,\varphi\vartheta).\qquad\qquad\text{($\varphi$ cocart.)}

Proposition 88

(Cocartesian fibrations are cotensored over maps/shape inclusions)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family with associated projection
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
. For any type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, the maps
\pi^{X}
and
\pi^{Y}
are cocartesian fibrations, and moreover the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{Y}}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.30
.

By
62
,
P^{X}
and
P^{Y}
are cocartesian families with associated projections
\pi^{X}
and
\pi^{Y}
, resp. Furthermore, said proposition tells us that cocartesian lifts in exponentials are computed pointwise. Invoking fibrant replacement and the axiom of choice, this gives the following.

Lifts in the cocartesian fibration

E^{Y}\simeq\sum_{u:Y\to B}\prod_{y:Y}P(u(y)){\mathrel{\to}^{\pi^{Y}}}B^{Y}

are given by

(P^{Y})_{!}\Big(\alpha:u\to u^{\prime},f:\prod_{Y}u^{*}P\Big):\equiv\lambda y.P_{!}(\alpha_{y}:u_{y}\to u_{y}^{\prime},f_{y}).

Lifts in the cocartesian fibration

E^{X}\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:Y\to B\\
v:X\to B\end{subarray}}\sum_{p:j^{*}v=u}\sum_{f:\prod_{Y}u^{*}P}\sum_{g:\prod_{X}v^{*}P}j^{*}g=_{p}f{\mathrel{\to}^{\pi^{X}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:Y\to B\\
v:X\to B\end{subarray}}j^{*}v=u\simeq B^{X}

“sitting above” are given by

\displaystyle(P_{!})^{X}\Big(\alpha:u\to u^{\prime},f:\prod_{Y}u^{*}P,\beta:v\to v^{\prime},g:\prod_{X}v^{*}P,\varphi:j^{*}\beta=\alpha,r:j^{*}g=_{\varphi_{0}}f\Big)

\displaystyle:\equiv

\displaystyle\left\langle P_{!}^{Y}(\alpha,f),\lambda x.P_{!}(\beta_{x},g_{x}),\lambda y.P_{!}^{2}(\varphi_{y},r_{y})\right\rangle

where the
2
-dimensional lift of
\varphi
w.r.t.
r
is constructed as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{*}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{*}v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{*}v^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P^{Y})_{!}(j^{*}\beta,j^{*}g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P^{Y})_{!}(\alpha,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P^{Y})_{!}^{2}(\varphi,r)}

Since the functor
E^{j}
maps tuples
\langle u,v,p,f,g,r\rangle
in
E^{X}
to pairs
\langle u,f\rangle
in
E^{Y}
one can readily verify that it is cocartesian, using the computations above.

Proposition 89

(Cocartesian functors are closed under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. Then, given cocartesian fibrations
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
, and a cocartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

the square induced between the Leibniz cotensors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{Y}\times_{E^{Y}}E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}\times_{B^{Y}}B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Y}\times_{\pi^{Y}}\pi^{X}}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.31
.

This follows from using
88
and then applying the universal property
84
.

To sum up the results from
Section

5.2.3
and the ones above, our synthetic cocartesian families satisfy stability properties analogous to those of an
\infty
-cosmos, cf.
[
63
, Definition 1.2.1]
:

Proposition 90

(Cosmological closure properties of cocartesian families)
.

1.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Cocartesian families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always cocartesian.

2.

Between cocartesian families over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Cocartesian functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
32
32

32

Here, all three objectwise limit notions are meant to satisfy the expected universal properties
w.r.t. to cocartesian functors
.
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always cocartesian.

Proof 5.32
.

Cf. the previous statements, as well as
Section

5.2.3
, and
94
.

We end the section with yet another useful stability property.

Proposition 91

(Base change of cocartesian functors,
[
63
, Exercise 5.3.i]
)
.

Let
A,B
be a Rezk type and
j:A\to B
be a map.

Consider cocartesian families
P,P^{\prime}:B\to\mathcal{U}
and a cocartesian functor
\varphi:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,P^{\prime})
. Then the mediating map
\psi\equiv\lambda a,e.\langle ja,e\rangle:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{A}(j^{*}P,j^{*}P^{\prime})
is also a cocartesian functor.
We write
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\pi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B
for the fibrations corresponding to
P
,
P^{\prime}
, and similarly for the pullback fibrations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Proof 5.33
.

Let
v:a\to a^{\prime}
be an arrow in
A
, and
f:e\to_{jv}e^{\prime}
a dependent arrow in
P
. By definition, we have identifications:

\displaystyle\psi_{v}(f)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{jv}(f)

(5.11)

\displaystyle\psi_{a}(e)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{ja}(e)

(5.12)

Since
\varphi
is a cocartesian functor, there is a path

\displaystyle\varphi_{v}(P_{!}(v,e))=P_{!}^{\prime}(v,\varphi_{b}e).

(5.13)

The cocartesian lifts in the pullback family are given by

\displaystyle Q_{!}^{\prime}(v,e)=P_{!}^{\prime}(jv,e).

(5.14)

Taken together, this gives rise to an identification

\psi_{v}(Q_{!}(v,e))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:pb-fibw-f}}}{{=}}\varphi_{jv}(P_{!}(jv,e))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:pb-varphi-cocart}}}{{=}}P_{!}^{\prime}(jv,\varphi_{ja}(e))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:pb-fibw-e},\ref{eq:pb-lifts}}}{{=}}Q_{!}^{\prime}(v,\psi_{a}e).

5.3.4
Characterizations of cocartesian functors

Theorem 5.34

(
[
63
, Theorem 5.3.4]
)
.

Let

and

be Rezk types, and consider cocartesian families

and

with associated fibrations

and

, resp .

the following are equivalent:

1.

The fiberwise map

is a cocartesian functor.

2.

The mate of the induced natural isomorphism, fibered over

, is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
