The mate of the induced natural isomorphism is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof 5.35
.

Let
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
be the straightening of
\xi
and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
the straightening of
\pi
.

\ref{it:cocart-fun-cocart}\iff\ref{it:cocart-fun-mate-fibered}

: Consider the first (fibered) adjunction, where the mate of the canonical isomorphism cell is constructed through the following pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The unit
\eta:\hom_{\xi\downarrow A}(\id_{F},\kappa i)
at
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},d:P_{a}\rangle
is given as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(u,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

The pasting
2
-cell can be identified with the natural transformation

\alpha:\hom_{F\to\pi\downarrow B}(\kappa^{\prime}\circ\varphi\downarrow j,\varphi\circ\kappa)

whose components at
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},d:Pa\rangle
are given by the fillers:

\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}:\equiv(\kappa^{\prime}\circ\varphi\downarrow j)\eta_{\langle u,d\rangle}:\langle jd^{\prime},(ju)_{!}(\varphi d)\rangle\to\langle jd^{\prime},\varphi(u_{!}d)\rangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi(u_{!}d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ju)_{!}(\varphi d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j(a^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{!}(u,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\big(P_{!}(u,d)\big)}

If
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle
is a cocartesian functor there is an identification
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))=P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)
in
E^{\Delta^{1}}
, hence
\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}
is an identity.

On the other hand, if the induced filler
\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}
happens to be an isomorphism, and thus an identity, we obtain an identification
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))=P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)
rendering
\Phi
a cocartesian functor.

\ref{it:cocart-fun-cocart}\iff\ref{it:cocart-fun-mate}

: In case of the second adjunction, the mate is given by the cell constructed by pasting from the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}}

The counit
\varepsilon^{\prime}:\hom_{E^{\Delta^{1}}\to E^{\Delta^{1}}}(\ell^{\prime}\circ r^{\prime},\id_{E^{\Delta^{1}}})
at
\langle v:b\to b^{\prime},g:e\to e^{\prime}\rangle
is given by the fillers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\langle{v,g}\rangle}}

The pasting cell can be identified with the natural transformation

\beta:\hom_{\xi\downarrow A\to F^{\Delta^{1}}}(\varphi\downarrow j\circ\ell^{\prime},\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}\circ\ell)

whose components at
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},d:Pa\rangle
are given by:

\beta_{\langle u,d\rangle}\equiv\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}(\ell\langle u,d\rangle)}:\langle ju,P_{!}(ju,\varphi(d))\rangle\to\langle ju,\varphi Q_{!}(u,d)\rangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi(u_{!}d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ju)_{!}(\varphi d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{ja}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{ja^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ju}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\langle{u,d}\rangle}}

If
\Phi\equiv\langle\varphi,u\rangle
is a cocartesian functor, then
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))
is a cocartesian arrow, so the filler
\beta_{\langle u,d\rangle}
must be too, thus an identity.

Conversely,
\beta_{\langle u,d\rangle}
being an identity gives rise to an identification
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))=P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)
. This implies that
\Phi
is a cocartesian functor.

An application of the characterization theorem is a proof that any fibered left adjoint (over a common base) is a cocartesian functor.

Lemma 92

(
2
-cell conservativity for comma types, cf.
[
63
, Proposition 3.4.6(iii)]
)
.

Let
g:C\rightarrow A\leftarrow B:f
be a cospan of Rezk types. For generalized elements
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}:X\to f\downarrow g
, consider a natural transformation
\tau:\alpha\Rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}
. If
\partial_{k}\tau
for both
k=0,1
are natural isomorphisms, then
\tau
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof 5.36
.

We have an equivalence
X\to f\downarrow g\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{0}:X\to B\\
\alpha_{1}:X\to C\end{subarray}}\prod_{x:X}f\alpha_{0}x\to g\alpha_{1}x
. The natural transformation
\tau
at stage
x:X
is given by a square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\alpha_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\alpha^{\prime}_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g\alpha_{1}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g\alpha_{1}^{\prime}x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(\partial_{1}\tau_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\partial_{0}\tau_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{x}}

Invoking transformation extensionality, if both whiskered
2
-cells
\partial_{k}\tau_{x}
are isomorphisms, then by functoriality the squares
\tau_{x}
degenerate to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{x}}

exhibiting
\tau
as a natural isomorphism.

Proposition 93

(Fibered left adjoints are cocartesian functors,
[
63
, Lemma 5.3.6]
)
.

Consider a fibered functor between cocartesian fibrations where the horizontal arrows make up a fibered adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Then
\varphi
is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.37
.

Since
\xi
and
\pi
are cocartesian, we obtain the following induced diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\downarrow k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Specifically, the vertical adjunctions follow from the Chevalley criterion for cocartesian fibrations. The top horizontal adjunction exists because of
121
. The bottom horizontal adjunction exists by
127
.
We now have to show that the the mate w.r.t. the vertical adjunctions is invertible, which by
117
is equivalent to the mate w.r.t. the horizontal adjunctions being invertible. But this is established by
2
-cell conservativity of comma objects, cf.
92
.

Any fibered equivalence between arbitrary maps is also cocartesian.

Proposition 94

(Fibered equivalences are cocartesian functors)
.

For (cocartesian) fibrations
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, any square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof 5.38
.

By univalence we can replace anonymous equivalences by identities (
equivalence induction
), and these are easily seen to be cocartesian functors (since they preserve cocartesian lifts).

Alternatively, in the case of cocartesian fibrations, one could argue similarly as in
93
, cf.
[
63
, Corollary 5.3.1]

6
More on covariant families

Covariant families have been introduced and thoroughly analyzed by Riehl–Shulman, cf.
[
61
, Section 8]
. Since they can be characterized by a right orthogonality condition (namely w.r.t. the map
0:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
), it follows formally that they satisfy all the closure properties from Subsection
3.1.2
, yielding the analog of the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties, cf.
100
. As a perhaps rather non-obvious result, we prove that covariant families (over an arbitrary type) are automatically inner. Furthermore, we prove the expected result that, over a Rezk type, a family is covariant if and only if it is cocartesian and all its fibers are discrete. We also argue how covariant families can be seen as a type-theoretic analogue of discrete cocartesian fibrations in the
\infty
-cosmological sense.

The section concludes by establishing a directed version of the
encode-decode method

[
82
, Section 8.9]
, which we expect to be useful when analyzing localization or other higher inductive types in later work.

In the following section, the Yoneda Lemma for covariant families, originally established in
[
61
, Section 9]
, is recovered in
Section

7.2
as a special case of the Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian families.

6.1
Properties and characterizations of covariant families

6.1.1
Covariant vs. inner families

Proposition 95
.

Every covariant family is an inner family.

Since inner families are a relative version of Segal types the proposition at hand can be seen as a relative version of
[
61
, Proposition 7.3]
.

Proof 6.1
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a covariant family.
Suppose we are given a

2
-simplex
\sigma
in
B
with boundary given by

u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, and
w:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})
, for

b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B
,
i.e.
,
\sigma:\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)
. Furthermore, assume
there are dependent arrows
f:\hom^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
,

g:\hom^{P}_{v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
for
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
,
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}
.
We prove that there is a contractible choice of elements of the
extension type

\Big\langle\prod_{t_{2}\leq t_{1}}P(\sigma(t_{1},t_{2}))\Big|{}^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}_{[f,g]}\Big\rangle

(6.1)

by giving a
center
and by showing that any two elements are equal.
33
33

33

In general, a type
A
is contractible iff the type
\isContr(A)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{c:A}\lx@nobreakspace\prod_{x:A}(c=_{A}x)
is inhabited. For any pair
\langle c,H_{c}\rangle
we call
c:A
a center of contraction of
A
and
H_{c}
a contraction of
A
. It is common to strengthen the articles to be
definite
: contractible data is unique up to contractibility anyway, and this is the usual sense in which data in HoTT is deemed unique. We therefore allow ourselves to speak of
the
center of contraction of
A

etc.
, cf.
[
68
, Definition 10.1.1]
.

The center is obtained using the homotopy extension property (HEP),
cf.
[
61
, Proposition 4.10]
,
Section

2.1.2
. Since
P
is covariant, there is an element

H:\prod_{b_{1}:B}\prod_{u_{1}:\hom_{B}(b,b_{1})}\isContr\Big\langle\prod_{t}P(u_{1}(t))\Big|{}^{0}_{e}\Big\rangle.

To use the HEP, we first give an element of

\prod_{t_{2}\leq t_{1}}P(\sigma(t_{1},t_{2}))
. If
t_{2}
is given, consider

K(t_{2}):\equiv H\,\sigma(1,t_{2})\,(\lambda t.\,\sigma(t,t\land t_{2}))
. The corresponding center applied to
t_{1}
is in

P(\sigma(t_{1},t_{1}\land t_{2}))\equiv P(\sigma(t_{1},t_{2}))
. We now need
to provide a homotopy between this and
[f,g]
, but this is
obtained from the contraction part of
H
.

To show that any two elements
\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}

in (
6.1
) are equal, we use relative function
extensionality
[
61
, Proposition 4.8]
,
Section

2.1.2
, by which it suffices to give
an element of the extension type

\Big\langle\prod_{t_{2}\leq t_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})=\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\Big|{}^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}_{{\mathrm{refl}}}\Big\rangle

Again we appeal to HEP. Given
t_{2}\leq t_{1}
, from
K(t_{2})
we get an
identity between
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{2}
restricted to
the extension type
\langle\prod_{t}P(t,t\land t_{2})|{}^{0}_{e}\rangle
. From the
easy direction of relative function extensionality we get an
identity
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})=\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})
.
To show that the resulting homotopy is homotopic to
{\mathrm{refl}}
on

\Lambda^{2}_{1}
, we use tope disjunction elimination.

On the edge
t_{2}=0
we have an identity
f=f
in the contractible
type
\langle\prod_{t}P(\sigma(t,0))|{}^{0}_{e}\rangle
. But an identity
type in a contractible type is itself contractible, so this identity
is equal to
{\mathrm{refl}}
.

On the edge
t_{1}=1
we may consider an arbitrary
t_{2}
and use the
same argument.

6.1.2
Covariant vs. cocartesian families

Proposition 96
.

Any covariant family over a Rezk type
B
is a cocartesian family.

Proof 6.2
.

A family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant if and only if the Leibniz cotensor
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi_{P}:\widetilde{P}^{\Delta^{1}}\to B^{\Delta^{1}}\times_{B}\widetilde{P}
is an equivalence. By
23
this also constitutes an adjoint equivalence, and by fibrant replacement the left adjoint can be chosen a (strict) section.

Proposition 97
.

Let
B
be a Segal type. Assume
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is such that the following properties are satisfied:

1.

P
is an inner family.

2.

P
has the cocartesian lifting property.

3.

All fibers
P\,b
for
b:B
are discrete.

Then
P
is covariant.

Proof 6.3
.

Let
b,b^{\prime}:B
with
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
, and
e:P\,b
. There is
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
and a lift
f:\hom^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})
which is cocartesian, meaning that for any
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
and
g:\hom^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime\prime})
there is a unique arrow
h:\hom^{P}_{\id_{b^{\prime}}}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
such that
h\circ f=g
. This dependent composition becomes an ordinary composition in the total space
\widetilde{P}
which is Segal, since
B
is. Now, as
\hom^{P}_{\id_{b^{\prime}}}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\simeq\hom_{P\,b^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
, and
P\,b^{\prime}
is discrete,
h
can be taken to be an identity. But then
f=g
.

Corollary 98
.

A cocartesian family over a Segal type is covariant if and only if all its fibers are discrete.

Proposition 99
.

A cocartesian family is covariant if and only if all dependent arrows are cocartesian.

Proof 6.4
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family.

If
P
is covariant, then the unique lift for any
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
w.r.t.
e:P\,b
must be (identifiable with) a cocartesian arrow. Since any dependent arrow is given as a lift of some arrow in the base, it follows that any dependent arrow is cocartesian.

Conversely, if every dependent arrow in
P
is cocartesian, any two lifts of
u:b\to b^{\prime}
beginning at some
e:P\,b
become equal (since the filler induced by the universal property is exhibited as an isomorphism).

6.2
Closure properties of covariant families

Proposition 100

(Cosmological closure properties of covariant families)
.

1.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Covariant families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Furthermore, they are closed under sequential limits, and satisfy left cancelation. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always covariant.

2.

Between covariant families over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Fibered functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
34
34

34

all three objectwise limit notions satisfying the expected universal properties w.r.t. to fibered functors
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always covariant.

Proof 6.5
.

Most of the properties follow from the established results about cocartesian fibrations
90
, or the additional closure properties that hold for right classes. Recall that fibered functors between covariant families are automatically cocartesian.
We are only left with stability under objectwise pullback (which will imply stability under objectwise sequential limits). We assume a diagram as follows whose vertically drawn maps are covariant fibrations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}

We are to prove that the induced map
E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}
is a covariant fibration as well.
To this end, we observe the following. Given a triple of arrows
\langle u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\rangle
in
B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}
with points
\langle e_{0},e^{\prime}_{0},e^{\prime\prime}_{0}\rangle:E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}
lying over the respective domains the cocartesian lift is given fiberwise by

\langle P_{!}(u,e_{0}),P^{\prime}_{!}(u^{\prime},e_{0}^{\prime}),P^{\prime\prime}_{!}(u^{\prime\prime},e_{0}^{\prime\prime})\rangle.

Due to covariance all the components are uniquely determined (after projecting), and so the lift in
E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}
is as well.

6.3
Covariant families as discrete objects

In
\infty
-cosmos theory, covariant families arise as a kind of discrete objects. Their analogues in simplicial type theory satisfy a corresponding property as well.

Namely, over a fixed base, a discrete cocartesian fibration in an
\infty
-cosmos is defined as a cocartesian fibration (relative to the given
\infty
-cosmos) that is required to be discrete as an object of the slice
\infty
-cosmos
[
63
, Proposition 1.2.22]
. There are two equivalent ways of expressing the latter. In our current type-theoretic setting, in absence of categorical universes, we cannot give a systematic account of these definitions, but we can still give appropriate translations of both of these criteria, and prove they are satisfied by discrete covariant families.

To state these principles, we need to type-theoretically capture some of the structure of sliced
\infty
-cosmoses, namely sliced versions of function types as well as simplicial cotensors.

The enrichment over categories is simply given by the object of fiberwise maps (cf.
Section

5.3.2
), itself a Rezk type:

Definition 101

(Sliced function type,
[
63
, Proposition 1.2.22(ii)]
)
.

For two maps
\pi:E\to B
,
\xi:F\to B
over a common base type
B
, the
sliced function type (over
B
)
is given by the pullback object:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\xi,\pi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Taking the sliced cotensor amounts to taking the usual exponential of each fiber:

Definition 102

(Sliced cotensor,
[
63
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be a map, and
X
be a type or shape. The
sliced exponential (over
B
) of
\pi
by
X
is given by the map
X\boxtimes E\to B
defined as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

If
X
is a shape
\Phi
, we can take the strict extension type
{\Phi\boxtimes E}
which is fibered equivalent to the dependent pair type along the powers of the fibers,
i.e.
, over
B
, we have an equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{b:B}\Phi\to P\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
denotes the family of fibers of
\pi
.

In particular, the sliced exponential w.r.t.
\Phi\equiv\Delta^{1}
has as total type the type of vertical arrows.

Proposition 103

(Covariant families are cosmologically discrete, cf.
[
63
, Definition 5.5.3]
)
.

Over a Rezk type, for a cocartesian family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
with associated projection
\pi:E\to B
the following are equivalent:

1.

The map
\pi
is
i_{0}
-orthogonal.

2.

The map induced by the non-degenerate map
s:\Delta^{1}\to\mathbb{E}
is a fibered equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{1}\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

3.

For all global elements
b:\mathbf{1}\to B
, the sliced function type
\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(b,\pi)
is discrete.

Furthermore, if
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a covariant family, then, for any map
\xi:F\to B
, the sliced function type
\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\xi,\pi)
is discrete.

Proof 6.6
.

\ref{it:disc-orth}\iff\ref{it:disc-shape}
:

Criterion
2
is equivalent to
\prod_{b:B}\isEquiv(s\to P\,b)
,
s\to P\,b:(\mathbb{E}\to P\,b)\to(\Delta^{1}\to P\,b)
, which precisely says that all fibers of
P
are discrete. Given that
P
is cocartesian, this is equivalent to
P
being covariant by
98
.

\ref{it:disc-orth}\iff\ref{it:disc-fun}
:

Similarly, since
\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(b,\pi)\simeq P\,b
, Criterion
3
is also equivalent to
P
being covariant.

Now, assume
P
is covariant. Given a map
\xi:F\to B
, the exponential
\pi^{F}:E^{F}\to B^{F}
is a covariant fibration, too, hence the fiber
\xi^{*}\pi^{F}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\xi,\pi)
is discrete.

6.4
Directed encode-decode

We point out a directed version of the
“encode-decode method” in order to characterize

\hom
-types in higher inductive types
(and localizations).

The encode-decode method is used for a type
A
with
a:A
,
together with a family
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
with
d:P(a)
.
The elimination rule for identity types gives a fiberwise
map
\varphi:\prod_{x:A}(a=x\to P(x))
,
sending the reflexivity path to
d
.
Since
\sum_{x:A}a=x
is contractible,
and a fiberwise map is a fiberwise equivalence if and only if
the map on total types is an equivalence,
we get that
\varphi
is a fiberwise equivalence
if and only if
\sum_{x:A}P(x)
is contractible.

This has the following directed analog:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
A
be a Segal type with
a:A
, and let

P:A\to\mathcal{U}
be a covariant family with
d:P(a)
.
The fiberwise map

\varphi:\prod_{x:A}(\hom_{A}(a,x)\to P(x))

given by
\varphi(x,f):=f_{!}\,x
,
is a fiberwise equivalence if and only if

\langle a,d\rangle
is an initial object in
\sum_{x:A}P(x)
.
35
35

35

Recall that
\sum_{x:A}P(x)
is a Segal type.

Proof 6.8
.

By
[
61
, Lemma 9.8]
,
\sum_{x:A}\hom_{A}(a,x)
has
\langle a,\id_{a}\rangle

as initial object.

Conversely, if
\langle a,d\rangle
is initial in
\sum_{x:A}P(x)
,
then each type
\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(a,x)}{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{f}(d,e)

is contractible, where
x:A
and
e:P(x)
.
By
[
61
, Lemma 8.13]
,
we have equivalences
{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{f}(d,e)\simeq(f_{!}\,d=e)
,
and hence we get that the fiber of
\varphi_{x}
at
e
,
which is
\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(a,x)}(f_{!}\,d=e)
,
is equivalent to
\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(a,x)}{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{f}(d,e)
,
and is hence contractible.

7
Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian families

In our synthetic setting, we prove a version of the Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian fibrations which reads as follows: For a Rezk type
B
and a cocartesian family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, evaluation at the identity arrow
is an equivalence:

\prod_{b:B}\isEquiv\left(\Big(\prod^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{u:b\mathbin{\downarrow}B}P(\partial_{1}\,u)\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{b}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\,b\right)

Generalizing work by Street
[
80
]
, this Yoneda Lemma has been formulated and proved by Riehl–Verity w.r.t.
\infty
-cosmoses
[
63
, Theorem 5.7.3]
, showing that it applies to cocartesian fibrations of
(\infty,n)
-categories for
0\leq n\leq\infty
. For our proof, we adapt Riehl–Verity’s methods to the type-theoretic setting, with significant simplifications because we are only considering
(\infty,1)
-categories. At the same time, this generalizes Riehl–Shulman’s type-theoretic Yoneda Lemma for
discrete
covariant fibrations
[
61
, Theorems 9.1, 9.5]
.
36
36

36

In the discrete covariant case, the statement reads exactly the same, only without the restriction to cocartesian sections which has become vacuous.
A semantic version of the fibered Yoneda Lemma for discrete (co-)cartesian fibrations over Segal spaces has also been established by Rasekh
[
57
, Theorem 3.49]
.

As discussed by Riehl–Shulman, the synthetic Yoneda Lemma can be understood as a
directed
path induction principle, now also applying to the case of
categorical
fibers. In fact—following Riehl–Verity as well as Riehl–Shulman—the Yoneda Lemma will be implied by a general result about cocartesian fibrations
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a base
B
with initial element
b:B
. Namely, evaluation at
b
induces a LARI adjunction between the fiber and the type of sections which yields a (quasi-)equivalence when restricted to the cocartesian sections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{B}P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This is discussed in
Section

7.1
. The Yoneda Lemma will follow as an instance of this result, which gets discussed in
Section

7.2
.

Here, for a given
b:B
, the LARI
\mathbf{y}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathbf{y}_{b}
is defined at
d:P\,b
,
x:B
via cocartesian transport as

(\mathbf{y}\,d)(x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\partial_{1}\,P_{!}(\emptyset_{x},d).

A key step is showing that
\mathbf{y}
lands in
cocartesian
sections. This can be shown by interpreting cocartesian lifts as
2
-cells
\chi_{d}:\mathrm{cst}\,d\rightarrow\mathbf{y}\,d
, and then concluding by the formal properties of cocartesian arrows that the components of
\mathbf{y}\,d
are, in fact, cocartesian arrows.

7.1
Cocartesian sections from initial elements

Recall the definition of an initial element in a type.

Definition 104

(Initial element,
[
61
, Definition 9.6]
)
.

Let
B
be a type. An element
b:B
is
initial
if

\prod_{a:B}\isContr(\hom_{B}(b,a)).

We write
\emptyset_{a}:\hom_{B}(b,a)
for the center of contraction if
b:B
is initial. First, we establish the LARI adjunction result between the sections of the family and the fiber at the initial element.

Proposition 105

(
[
63
, Proposition 5.7.13]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family over a Rezk type
B
. If
b:B
is initial the evaluation functor
\mathrm{ev}_{b}:\prod_{B}P\to P\,b
has a LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{B}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}}
\vdash

Proof 7.1
.

The candidate for the left adjoint is

\mathbf{y}:P\,b\to\prod_{B}P,\quad\mathbf{y}:\equiv\lambda d.\lambda x.{(\emptyset_{x})}_{!}(d)\equiv\lambda d.\lambda x.\partial_{1}\,{P}_{!}({\emptyset_{x}},{d}).

We will show that the map

\varphi:\hom_{\prod_{B}P}(\mathbf{y}\,d,\sigma)\to\hom_{P\,b}(d,\sigma\,b),\quad\varphi:\equiv\lambda\kappa.\kappa\,b

is an equivalence.

Since
b
is initial, for any
x:B
, the hom-type
\hom_{B}(b,x)
is contractible with center
\emptyset_{x}
. Since
P
is cocartesian, we can define the map

\psi:\hom_{P\,b}(d,\sigma\,b)\to\hom_{\prod_{B}P}(\mathbf{y}\,d,\sigma),\psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda f.\lambda x.\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(\emptyset_{x},d)}(\sigma(\emptyset_{x})\circ f).

To illustrate,
\psi
yields the right-hand vertical arrow in squares of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{y}d)x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(\emptyset_{x},d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi(f,x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}

where
f:\hom_{P\,b}(d,\sigma\,b)
. For the round-trip along through
\varphi
and
\psi
, note that we obtain a path
\varphi(\psi f)=\id_{b}
since cocartesian lifts of identities are again identities.

For the other direction, note that as described in
Section

5.3.1
a morphism of sections
\kappa:\hom_{\prod_{B}P}(\mathbf{y}\,d,\sigma)
yields a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{y}d)x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(\emptyset_{x},d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}

for every
x:B
.
But this means
\psi(\varphi\kappa)(x)=\kappa(x)
as desired.

One checks that
(\mathbf{y}f)(b)=f
, so
\mathbf{y}
is indeed a left adjoint right inverse to
\mathrm{ev}_{b}
.

In fact, the transport map
\mathbf{y}:P\,b\to\prod_{B}P
is valued in cocartesian sections. We prove this by a
2
-dimensional naturality property of the cocartesian lifts.

Proposition 106

(
[
63
, Proposition 5.7.18]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family and
b:B
be an initial object. The map

\mathbf{y}:P\,b\to\prod_{B}P,\quad\mathbf{y}:\equiv\lambda d.\lambda x.{(\emptyset_{x})}_{!}(d)

factors through the subtype of cocartesian sections,
i.e.
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{B}P.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}}

Proof 7.2
.

For
d:P\,b
, consider the constant map
\mathrm{cst}(d):\equiv\lambda x.d:B\to E
, where
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
. The cocartesian lifts w.r.t.
d:P\,b
give rise to a natural transformation

\chi:\hom_{B\to E}(\mathrm{cst}(d),\mathbf{y}d)\simeq\prod_{x:B}\hom_{E}(\mathrm{cst}(d,x),\mathbf{y}(d,x)),\quad\chi_{x}:\equiv{P}_{!}({\emptyset_{x}},{d}).

Given
x,x^{\prime}:B
, for any arrow
u:\hom_{B}(x,x^{\prime})
we want to argue that the action of
\chi
on
u
yields a cocartesian arrow
\chi_{u}:{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(\mathbf{y}dx,\mathbf{y}dx^{\prime})
.
37
37

37

We suppress the canonical
2
-cell witnessing composition.
Considering the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}(d,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}(d,x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}du}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

it follows that
\mathbf{y}du
is cocartesian by
50
(
2
).

Proposition 107

(
[
63
, Theorem 5.7.13]
,
[
61
, Theorem 9.7]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
b:B
an initial object, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family. Then evaluation at
b

\mathrm{ev}_{b}:\Big(\prod_{B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}P\Big)\to P\,b

is an equivalence.

Proof 7.3
.

By
106
, the map
\mathbf{y}
restricts to cocartesian sections. By
105
,
\mathrm{ev}_{b}\circ\mathbf{y}=\id_{P\,b}
.

We set
T:\equiv\prod_{B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}P
. For the converse direction, we define a natural transformation

\displaystyle\varepsilon:\hom_{T\to T}(\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{b},\id_{T})

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{\sigma:T}\hom_{T}(\mathbf{y}(\mathrm{ev}_{b})(\sigma),\sigma)

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{\sigma:T}\prod_{x:B}\hom_{P\,x}(\mathbf{y}(\sigma(b))(x),\sigma(x))

as follows:
For
x:B
, the action of
\sigma
on
\emptyset_{x}:\hom_{B}(b,x)
yields a cocartesian arrow
\sigma(\emptyset_{x}):{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{\emptyset_{x}}(\sigma(b),\sigma(x))
.

We define
\varepsilon_{\sigma,x}
as the following filler:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}(\sigma(b),x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(\emptyset_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P}_{!}({\emptyset_{x}},{\sigma(b)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\sigma,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}

By
50
(
2
),
\varepsilon_{\sigma,x}
is cocartesian. As a cocartesian lift of an identity, it is itself an identity. By
[
61
, Proposition 10.3]
,
\varepsilon:\hom_{T\to T}(\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{b},\id_{T})
is an identity.

7.2
Dependent and absolute Yoneda Lemma

We now obtain analogues of the (dependent) Yoneda Lemma as in
[
63
]
,
[
61
]
for cocartesian families.

Lemma 108

(
[
61
, Lemma 9.8]
)
.

Let
B
be a Segal type. For any element
b:B
, the identity morphism
\id_{b}:b\downarrow B
is an initial object.

Theorem 7.4

(Dependent Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian families, cf.
[
63
, Theorem 5.7.2]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
b:B
any element, and
Q:b\downarrow B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family. Then evaluation at
\id_{b}
as in

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{b}}:\Big(\prod_{b\downarrow B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}Q\Big)\to Q(\id_{b})

is an equivalence.

Proof 7.5
.

Because of
108
(cf.
[
61
, Lemma 9.8]
) this follows from
107
.

Theorem 7.6

(Yoneda Lemma for cocartesian families, cf.
[
63
, Theorem 5.7.3]
&
[
61
, Theorem 9.1]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
b:B
any element, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family. Then evaluation at
\id_{b}
as in

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{b}}:\Big(\prod_{b\downarrow B}^{\mathrm{cocart}}\partial_{1}^{*}P\Big)\to P\,b

is an equivalence, where

\partial_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u.u(1):b\downarrow B\to B.

Proof 7.7
.

This is an instance of the Dependent Yoneda Lemma
Theorem

7.4
for
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\partial_{1}^{*}P\equiv\lambda u.P(u1):b\downarrow B\to B
.

Noting that for a covariant family any section is cocartesian, we recover the discrete versions from
[
63
,
61
]
.

Corollary 109

(Dependent Yoneda Lemma for covariant families, cf.
[
61
, Theorem 9.5]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
b:B
any element, and
Q:b\downarrow B\to\mathcal{U}
a covariant family. Then evaluation at
\id_{b}
as in

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{b}}:\Big(\prod_{b\downarrow B}Q\Big)\to Q(\id_{b})

is an equivalence.

Corollary 110

(Yoneda Lemma for covariant families, cf.
[
63
, Theorem 5.7.1]
,
[
61
, Theorem 9.1]
,
[
32
, Proposition 2.1.7]
,
[
57
, Theorem 3.49]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
b:B
any element, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a covariant family. Then evaluation at
\id_{b}
as in

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{b}}:\Big(\prod_{b\downarrow B}\partial_{1}^{*}P\Big)\to P\,b

is an equivalence, where

\partial_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u.u(1):b\downarrow B\to B.

Appendix A
Lax squares

Since Segal types form an exponential ideal, simplicial type theory in fact captures some of the
2
-dimensional theory of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories, cf.
[
61
, Section 6]
. For Rezk types
A,B
the type
B^{A}
is again Rezk, with mapping spaces
\underset{A\to B}{\hom}(f,g)\equiv(f\Rightarrow g)
. This invites a treatment of
lax squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

which are encoded by
2
-cells

\sigma:hk\Rightarrow gf\simeq\prod_{a:A}(hk)(a)\to(gf)(a).

We are mainly interested in some basic results about mates, known from classical
2
-category theory, re-interpreted in the context of higher categories as in
[
63
]
(cf.
op. cit.
, Appendix B, for the classical setting). These occur in our study of cocartesian functors in
Section

5.3
.

First, in
Section

A.1
we explicitly define pasting operations for lax squares. Then we prove a simple pasting theorem for a specific pasting scheme, which is used in
Section

A.2
to establish the mates correspondence for an adjunction.

Alas, in the absence of categorical type universes, a general pasting theorem, possibly along the lines of
[
20
,
26
,
24
]
, seems out of reach. Therefore, we will only in an
ad hoc
manner consider a few very specific pasting schemes relevant to our specific applications, and prove well-definedness of these pastings by manual pasting diagram chases, which is straightforward, though lengthy to spell out.

A.1
Pasting of lax squares

In this subsection, we introduce horizontal and vertical pasting of lax squares of functors between Segal types. We prove that these pasting operations are associative, and establish a certain pasting theorem which will be used to establish the mates correspondence subsequently in
Section

A.2
.

Definition 111

(Pasting of squares)
.

1.

The
horizontal pasting
of a natural transformations
\alpha:\underset{A\to A^{\prime}}{\hom}(hu,u^{\prime}k)
with a natural transformation
\alpha^{\prime}:\underset{A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime}}{\hom}(h^{\prime}u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime})
as indicated in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\scriptstyle\boxminus$}}\alpha}

is defined as:

\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(\alpha^{\prime}*\id_{k})\circ(\id_{h^{\prime}}*\alpha):\underset{B\to A^{\prime\prime}}{\hom}(h^{\prime}hu,u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k)

2.

The
vertical pasting
of a natural transformations
\beta:\underset{A\to A^{\prime}}{\hom}(f^{\prime}h,kf)
with a natural transformation
\beta^{\prime}:\underset{A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime}}{\hom}(f^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime},k^{\prime}f^{\prime})
as indicated in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rightsquigarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}\boxminus\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

is defined as:

\beta^{\prime}\boxminus\beta:\equiv(\id_{k^{\prime}}*\beta)\circ(\beta^{\prime}*\mathrm{id}_{h}):\underset{A\to B^{\prime\prime}}{\hom}(f^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}h,k^{\prime}kf)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}hu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}u^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}\boxminus\beta\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\scriptstyle\boxminus$}}\alpha\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}f^{\prime}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}kf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{h^{\prime}}*\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}*\mathrm{id}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}*\id_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{k^{\prime}}*\beta}

Figure 10:
Horizontal and vertical pasting of squares

Proposition 112

(Associativity of horizontal pasting of squares)
.

For Segal types, consider
2
-cells as given in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime\prime}}

This defines a unique pasting
2
-cell
\alpha^{\prime\prime\prime}:\hom_{B\to A^{\prime\prime\prime}}(h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}hu,u^{\prime\prime\prime}k^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k)
, so in particular there is an identification

(\alpha^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha^{\prime})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha=\alpha^{\prime\prime\prime}=\alpha^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}(\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha).

Proof A.1
.

We define
\beta:\hom_{B\to A^{\prime\prime}}(h^{\prime}hu,u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k)
as the composition
\beta\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha
,
i.e.
, for all
b:B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{b}:h^{\prime}hu(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}u^{\prime}k(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}\alpha_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}_{kb}}

Pasting
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
on the right gives the natural transformation
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\beta\equiv(\alpha^{\prime\prime}*\id_{k^{\prime}k})\circ(\id_{h^{\prime\prime}}*\beta)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}hu(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{\prime\prime\prime}k^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime}k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}u^{\prime}k(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}\beta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}\alpha_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime}_{kb}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{k^{\prime}kb}}

By symmetry, one can show that for
\gamma:\hom_{B^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime\prime}}(h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime\prime}k^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime})
defined as
\gamma:\equiv\alpha^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha^{\prime}
the composite
\gamma\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha\equiv(\gamma*\id_{k})\circ(\id_{h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}}*\alpha)
coincides with
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\beta
.

.

For Segal types, consider

The proof is completely analogous to the proof for the horizontal pasting.

A.2
Mates

We are now able to capture the mates correspondence of an adjunction in type theory.

Proposition 115
.

Consider adjunctions and morphisms between Rezk types as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}

Proof A.3

(Proof of
115
)
.

Consider the maps

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle:\underset{B\to A^{\prime}}{\hom}(hu,u^{\prime}k)\to\underset{B\to A^{\prime}}{\hom}(f^{\prime}h,kf),

\displaystyle\Phi(\alpha):\equiv\varepsilon^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\alpha\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\eta

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:\underset{A\to B^{\prime}}{\hom}(f^{\prime}h,kf)\to\underset{B\to A^{\prime}}{\hom}(hu,u^{\prime}k),

\displaystyle\Psi(\beta):\equiv\eta^{\prime}\boxminus\beta\boxminus\varepsilon.

By the previous pasting theorem, we obtain for
\alpha:hu\Rightarrow u^{\prime}k
an identification

\Psi(\Phi(\alpha))=\eta^{\prime}\boxminus\Phi(\alpha)\boxminus\varepsilon=(\eta^{\prime}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\varepsilon^{\prime})\boxminus\alpha\boxminus(\eta\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\boxminus$}}\varepsilon)=\alpha

invoking witnesses for the triangle identities, via
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
.

The round trip in the converse direction is analogous.

Proposition 116

(Invertibility of conjugates,
[

, Example B.3.5, Warning B.3.7]

.

-cell

and its mate

. Then

is invertible if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

Proof A.4
.

By precondition, we have the identification
\alpha:u=u^{\prime}
. Recall the uniqueness of left adjoints and all the accompanying data
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
. Since both
f\dashv u
,
f^{\prime}\dashv u^{\prime}
, also
f^{\prime}\dashv u
, but this implies there is an identification
f=f^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the counit
\varepsilon^{\prime}
can be replaced by
\varepsilon
.

Thus, the mate of
\alpha
at
a:A
is homotopic to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fufa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}u^{\prime}fa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\eta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\alpha_{fa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{fa}}

which is an isomorphism by one of the triangle identities.

Proposition 117

.

after considering the composites of the adjunctions.

Appendix B
LARI and fibered adjunctions

We complement Riehl–Shulman’s theory of adjunctions
[
61
, Section 10]
by a treatment of
left adjoint right inverse (LARI) adjunctions
. Hereby, we assume the types involved to be Rezk.

Specifically, we provide a characterization result
Theorem

B.1
along the lines of
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
, as well as various closure properties which imply the closure properties in
Sections

3.2.2
and
5.2.3
. In particular, by fibrant replacement, we can identify LARI adjunctions between Rezk types with adjunctions whose left adjoint is a section. As for the stability properties, LARI adjunctions (between Rezk types) are always closed under dependent products, composition and pullback. This in turn implies the same closure properties for LARI fibrations
Section

3.2.2
.

Finally, we briefly discuss fibered adjunctions between isoinner families over a Rezk type. These occur in the characterization theorems of cocartesian families,
Theorem

5.10
, and functors,
Theorem

5.34
.

B.1
Left adjoint right inverse (LARI) adjunctions

After providing a characterization of LARI adjunctions, similar to and relying on
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
, we prove a few closure properties. From these we derive corresponding closure properties of
j
-LARI fibrations (hence also cocartesian fibrations) in the main text, cf.
Section

3.2.2
.

B.1.1
Characterizations of LARI adjunctions

Definition 118

(Transposing LARI adjunction, cf.
[
61
, Definition 11.1]
)
.

A
transposing left adjoint right inverse adjunction
(or
LARI adjunction
for short) between Rezk types
A,B
consists of:

•

a transposing adjunction, consisting of functors
u:B\to A
,
f:A\to B
, and a family of equivalences
\varphi:\prod_{a:A,b:B}\hom_{B}(fa,b)\simeq\hom_{A}(a,ub)

•

such that for all
a:A
the components
\eta_{\varphi,a}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi_{a,fa}(\id_{fa}):\hom_{A}(a,ufa)
of the unit are isomorphisms.

Given this data,
f
is called a
transposing left adjoint right inverse
(or
transposing LARI
).

Definition 119

(Bi-diagrammatic LARI adjunction, cf.
[
61
, Definition 11.6]
)
.

A
bi-diagrammatic left adjoint right inverse adjunction
(or
bi-diagrammatic LARI adjunction
) between Rezk types
A
and
B
consists of:

•

functors
u:B\to A
,
f:A\to B

•

a natural isomorphism
\eta:{\mathrm{iso}}_{A\to A}(\id_{A},uf)

•

two natural transformations
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}:\hom_{B\to B}(fu,\id_{B})

•

a path
\alpha:u\varepsilon\circ\eta u=\id_{u}

•

a path
\beta:\varepsilon^{\prime}f\circ f\eta=\id_{f}

Given this data,
f
is called a
bi-diagrammatic left adjoint right inverse
(or
bi-diagrammatic LARI
).

Definition 120

(Lifting LARI adjunction, cf.
[
66
, Proposition 4.4.12]
)
.

A
lifting LARI adjunction
between Rezk types
A
and
B
consists of functors
u:B\to A
,
f:A\to B
with a homotopy
\sigma:u\circ f=\id_{A}
such that the following holds: For all
a:A,b:B
and arrows
\alpha:\hom_{A}(a,ub)
there exists uniquely up to homotopy an arrow
\beta:\hom_{B}(fa,b)
with
u\circ\beta=\alpha
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[fa,b]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Given this data,
f
is called a
lifting left adjoint right inverse
(or
lifting LARI
).

Theorem B.1

(Characterizations of LARI adjunctions, cf.
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
)
.

For a functor
u:B\to A
between Rezk types the following types are equivalent propositions:

1.

The type of transposing LARIs of
u
.

2.

The type of lifting LARIs of
u
.

3.

The type of functors
f:A\to B
together with natural isomorphisms
\eta:{\mathrm{iso}}_{A\to A}(\id_{A},uf)
such that
\varphi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda k.uk\circ\eta_{a}:\hom_{B}(fa,b)\to\hom_{A}(a,ub)
is an equivalence for all
a:A
,
b:B
.

4.

The type of bi-diagrammatic LARIs of
u
.

Proof B.2
.

Straightening
u
to be the first projection of a family
P:A\to\mathcal{U}

(so that
B\simeq\widetilde{P}
),
and strictifying all the occurring data, shows that the types in
Item

2
and
Item

3
each are equivalent to the type

\sum_{f:\prod_{a:A}P\,a}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y:A\\
e:P\,y\end{subarray}}\prod_{\alpha:x\to y}\isContr\big(fx\to_{\alpha}e\big).

By
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
, the types of transposing and bi-diagrammatic left adjoints are equivalent to each other and to the type

\sum_{f:A\to B}\sum_{\eta:\id_{A}\Rightarrow uf}\isEquiv(\varphi_{\eta}).

Hence, the type

\sum_{f:A\to B}\sum_{\eta:\id_{A}\Rightarrow uf}\isEquiv(\varphi_{\eta})\times\isIso(\eta)

is equivalent to the type of bi-diagrammatic LARIs of
u
, which establishes equivalences between the types from
Items

3
,
4
and
1
. Since the type of bi-diagrammatic left adjoints is a proposition by
[
61
, Theorem 11.23]
, also the sub-type of bi-diagrammatic LARIs in
Item

4
is (and consequently, the type in
Item

2
is as well).

We remark that, in general, given two functors
u:B\to A
and

f:A\to B
, whether they determine an adjunction is extra
structure, and not just a proposition, but it is a proposition given
the data that
f
is a section of
u
(the proposition being: are the
corresponding transposing maps invertible?).
Similarly, whether
f
is a section of
u
is in general extra
structure, but this becomes a proposition when we have the data
of an adjunction (the proposition being: are the units invertible?).

B.1.2
Closure properties of LARI adjunctions

Proposition 121

((LARI) adjunctions are closed under products)
.

Let
I:\mathcal{U}
be a type, and
A,B:I\to\mathcal{U}
families with maps
r_{i}:B_{i}\to A_{i}
.
If there is, for every
i:I
, a (LARI) adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(i)}
\vdash

we get an induced (LARI) adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{I}\ell}
\vdash

Proof B.3
.

By fibrant replacement we consider the straightenings
P_{i}:A_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
, so that
B_{i}\simeq\widetilde{A_{i}}\simeq\sum_{a:A_{i}}P_{i}(a)
. Writing
\ell_{i}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle f_{i},L_{i}\rangle
, the induced map is given by

\prod_{i}\ell_{i}:\prod_{i}A_{i}\to\prod_{i}B_{i},\quad(\prod_{i}\ell_{i})(\alpha)\equiv\lambda i.\langle f_{i}\,\alpha_{i},L_{i}\,\alpha_{i}\rangle.

With this, we obtain

\displaystyle\hom_{\prod_{i}B_{i}}(\prod_{i}\ell_{i}\,\alpha,\langle\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{i}\hom_{B_{i}}(\ell_{i}(\alpha_{i}),\langle\alpha_{i}^{\prime},\beta_{i}^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{i}\hom_{B_{i}}(\langle f_{i}\alpha_{i},L_{i}\alpha_{i}\rangle,\langle\alpha_{i}^{\prime},\beta_{i}^{\prime},)\rangle

\displaystyle\simeq\prod_{i}\hom_{A_{i}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}^{\prime})

(
since
\ell_{i}\dashv r_{i}
)

\displaystyle\simeq\hom_{\prod_{i}A_{i}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}).

Thus, adjunctions are closed under taking dependent products. Clearly, this property descends to LARI adjunctions,
i.e.
, the case where
f_{i}:A_{i}\to A_{i}
is the identity.

Proposition 122

(LARI adjunctions are closed under pullback)
.

LARI adjunctions between Rezk types are stable under pullback,
i.e.
, given a map
r:C\to A
between Rezk types together with a LARI
\ell:A\to C
and a map
j:B\to A
where
B
is a Rezk type, then the map
r^{\prime}:\equiv j^{*}r
has a LARI as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof B.4
.

We fibrantly replace the square as follows. Denote by
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
the family of fibers of the map
j:B\to A
, so that
B\simeq\widetilde{P}
, and likewise,
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
the family associated to
r:C\to A
, so that
C\simeq\widetilde{Q}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{a:A}P\,a\times Q\,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{a:A}Q\,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{a:A}P\,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The section
\ell^{\prime}
is induced by the section
\ell
by setting
\ell^{\prime}:\equiv\lambda\langle a,d\rangle.\langle a,d,\ell(a)\rangle
.
39
39

39

Here, in the notation we are identifying sections with their “principal parts”.

The adjunction
\ell\dashv r
is given by an equivalence

\sum_{\alpha:a\to a^{\prime}}\ell(a)\to_{\alpha}e^{\prime}\simeq\hom_{C}(\langle a,\ell(a)\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\simeq}}\hom_{A}(a,a^{\prime})

giving rise to an equivalence

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha:a\to a^{\prime}}(d\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime})\times(\ell(a)\to e^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{D}(\langle a,d,\ell(a)\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{\prime}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\hom_{B}(\langle a,d\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime}\rangle)\simeq\sum_{\alpha:a\to a^{\prime}}d\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime},

in sum establishing that
\ell^{\prime}
is a left adjoint right inverse of
r^{\prime}
.

Proposition 123

(LARI adjunctions are closed under composition)
.

Any two (LARI) adjunctions between Rezk types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\vdash
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\vdash

compose to a (LARI) adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\ell}
\vdash

Proof B.5
.

By fibrant replacement, we take
r
and
r^{\prime}
, resp., to be the projections of families
P:A\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
, where
B:\equiv\widetilde{P}
and
C\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
d:P\,a\end{subarray}}Q\,a\,d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{a:A}P\,a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

We denote the actions of the maps
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
in the following way:

\displaystyle\ell\equiv\lambda a.

\displaystyle\langle\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a\rangle

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}\equiv\lambda\langle a,d\rangle.

\displaystyle\langle\ell_{0}^{\prime}\,a\,d,\ell_{1}^{\prime}\,a\,d,\ell_{2}^{\prime}\,a\,d\rangle

The adjunctions
\ell\dashv r
and
\ell^{\prime}\dashv r^{\prime}
are given by equivalences

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha:\ell_{0}a\to a^{\prime}}(\ell_{1}a\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{B}(\ell(a),\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime}\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\simeq}}\hom_{A}(a,a^{\prime})

(B.1)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:\ell^{\prime}_{0}(a,d)\to a^{\prime}\\
\beta:\ell_{1}^{\prime}(a,d)\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(\ell^{\prime}_{2}(a,d)\to_{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}e^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{C}(\ell^{\prime}(a,d),\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{\prime}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\hom_{B}(\langle a,d\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime}\rangle)\simeq\sum_{\alpha:a\to a^{\prime}}(d\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime}),

(B.2)

resp.
The composite of the left adjoints acts as:

\ell^{\prime\prime}\equiv\ell^{\prime}\circ\ell\equiv\lambda a.\langle\ell_{0}^{\prime}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a),\ell_{1}^{\prime}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a),\ell_{2}^{\prime}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a)\rangle

This gives rise to an equivalence witnessing the composite adjunction:

\displaystyle\hom_{C}(\ell^{\prime\prime}(a),\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:\ell^{\prime}_{0}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a)\\
\beta:\ell_{1}^{\prime}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a)\end{subarray}}\ell_{2}^{\prime}(\ell_{0}a,\ell_{1}a)\to_{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:adj-comp2}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha:\ell_{0}a\to a^{\prime}}\ell_{1}a\to_{\alpha}d^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:adj-comp1}}}{{\simeq}}\hom_{A}(a,a^{\prime})

Furthermore, clearly if both
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
, resp., happen to be sections of
r
and
r^{\prime}
, resp., then
\ell^{\prime}\circ\ell
is a section of
r^{\prime}\circ r
as can be seen from the above terms defining the functions.

B.1.3
Initial elements in a LARI adjunction

The following lemma is useful in our considerations of cocartesian arrows, in particular in proving their uniqueness up to homotopy (w.r.t. a fixed source vertex),
45
.

Recall first the discussion in
21
.

Lemma 124
.

Suppose
F\dashv U:E\to B
is a LARI adjunction of Segal types,
and let
b:B
. Then
b
is initial in
B
if and only if
F\,b

is initial in
E
.

Proof B.6
.

The implication from left to right is clear, since
left adjoints preserve initial objects.
40
40

40

We expect there to be a nice general theory of (co)limits
in simplicial type theory, but here we only need this specific case.

Conversely, for any
b^{\prime}:B
, since
F
is fully faithful
we have
\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\simeq\hom_{E}(F\,b,F\,b^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{1}
,
assuming
F\,b
is initial in
E
.

B.2
Fibered adjunctions

We give a brief treatment of fibered adjunctions which appear in the characterization theorems of cocartesian families and functors, resp.,
Theorems

5.10
and
5.34
. We furthermore show that fibered adjunctions over a common base pull back along arbitrary functors, which is used in the main text to show that, for a fibered adjunction between cocartesian fibrations, the fibered left adjoint is always cocartesian, cf.
93
.

Throughout the subsection, we assume all types to be Rezk.

Definition 125

(Fibered natural transformation)
.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
be maps. If
\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle k,\varphi\rangle
and
\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle m,\psi\rangle
each are fibered functors from
\xi
to
\pi
, then a
fibered natural transformation
\Phi
to
\Psi
consists of a pair of natural transformations
\mu:k\Rightarrow m
and
\vartheta:\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
as indicated in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

together with a family of paths

\prod_{a:A,d:Q\,a}\pi(\vartheta_{d})=\mu_{\xi_{d}}.

By fibrant replacement, writing
P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\pi)
and
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{A}(\xi)
, any fibered natural transformation can be presented by the data

•

\mu:\hom_{A\to B}(k,m)

•

\vartheta:\prod_{\langle a,d\rangle:F}\varphi(d)\longrightarrow_{\mu_{a}}^{P}\psi(d)
.

We also write shorthand
\vartheta:\varphi\Rightarrow_{\mu}\psi
.

Over a common base, we obtain that a natural transformation as given in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

is fibered if and only if all components of
\vartheta
are vertical arrows (cf.
58
).

Definition 126

(Fibered adjunctions,
[
63
, Definition 3.6.5]
)
.

Let
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be maps between Rezk types, and fibered functors
\varphi:\xi\to_{B}\pi
,
\psi:\pi\to_{B}\xi
. A
fibered adjunction (with fibered left adjoint
\psi
and fibered right adjoint
\varphi
)
is an adjunction
\psi\dashv\varphi
such that for all
\langle b,e\rangle:E
, the components
\eta_{b,e}
of the unit are vertical arrows. We indicate this by writing
\psi\dashv_{B}\varphi
or the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proposition 127

(Base change of fibered adjunctions, cf.
[
63
, Lemma 3.6.6(i), Exercise 5.3.i]
)
.

Given a fibered adjunction
\psi\dashv_{B}\varphi:F\to_{B}E
between isoinner fibrations
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow A
,
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, where
P\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\pi)
,
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\St_{B}(\xi)
, for any map
k:A\to B
the induced adjunction
\psi^{\prime}\dashv_{B}\varphi^{\prime}:k^{*}F\to_{B}k^{*}E
is again a fibered adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof B.7
.

Suppose given a fibered adjunction
\psi\dashv_{B}\varphi
as indicated with unit

\langle\id_{b},\eta_{e}\rangle:\langle b,e\rangle\to\langle b,(\varphi\psi)_{b}(e)\rangle,

for
b:B
,
e:P\,b
.
By assumption, for
b^{\prime}:B
,
d^{\prime}:Q\,d^{\prime}
, this induces an equivalence

\Phi_{\eta}:\hom_{F}(\langle b,\psi_{b}(e)\rangle,\langle b^{\prime},d^{\prime}\rangle)\to\hom_{E}(\langle b,e\rangle,\langle b^{\prime},\varphi_{b}^{\prime}(d^{\prime})\rangle)

by

\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,g.\langle u,\varphi_{u}(g)\circ\eta_{e}\rangle.

For the unit of the adjunction over
A
we take

\langle\id_{a},\eta_{e}\rangle:\langle a,e\rangle\to\langle a,(\psi\varphi)_{ka}(e)\rangle

for
a:A
,
e:P(ka)
. This gives rise to the transposing map

\Phi^{\prime}_{\eta}:\hom_{k^{*}F}(\langle a,\psi_{ka}(e)\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},d^{\prime}\rangle)\to\hom_{k^{*}E}(\langle a,e\rangle,\langle a^{\prime},\varphi_{b}^{\prime}(d^{\prime})\rangle)

for
a^{\prime}:A
,
d^{\prime}:Q(ka^{\prime})
.
Contractibility of the fiber of a pair
\langle v:a\to a^{\prime},h:e\to_{kv}^{P}\varphi_{b}^{\prime}(d^{\prime})\rangle
demands the unique existence of an arrow
g_{h}:\psi_{ka}(e)\to^{P}_{v}d^{\prime}
such that

\varphi_{kv}(g_{h})\circ\eta_{e}=h,

which follows from the respective condition for the original map
\Phi
.
